#5129. Deutsche Bank Goes Live With Crypto Custody After a Five-Year Buildup

Deutsche Bank Goes Live With Crypto Custody After a Five-Year Buildup

Five years after it first floated the idea, Germany’s largest lender is now holding Bitcoin for clients.

Deutsche Bank said Wednesday that its Digital Asset Custody service has gone live for paying clients, giving institutional and corporate customers in Europe a regulated way to store and transfer Bitcoin, Ethereum and three stablecoins: Circle’s USDC and EURC, plus EURAU.

The bank manages wallets and private keys on clients’ behalf. For now, the service covers custody and transfers, with tokenized financial instruments on its roadmap.

The service is aimed at clients of Deutsche Bank’s corporate and investment banking units, including corporations, asset managers, hedge funds, custodians, brokers and sovereign institutions, according to the bank’s September announcement of the plan.

Its setup includes hardware-based key protection, multi-person approvals and separate warm and cold storage, the bank said.

“In the medium term we aim to deliver a fully integrated, regulated ecosystem that supports the custody, tokenisation, and settlement of digital assets,” said Paul Maley, Deutsche Bank’s global head of trust and securities services.

Deutsche Bank also signed a memorandum of understanding with Deutsche Asset Management, the majority-owned money manager formerly known as DWS, to explore working together on tokenized money market funds, crypto exchange-traded commodities and stablecoins.

The asset manager oversaw about 1.22 trillion euros as of Sept. 30. The bank has also said it plans to expand the custody service to Asia.

The launch caps a long road, going back to at least 2021 when Deutsche Bank began readying a crypto custody and trading platform. Then, in 2023, the bank applied for a digital asset custody license in Germany. Last year, reports said the bank planned to build the service with technology from crypto exchange Bitpanda, alongside its longtime partner Taurus.

Deutsche Bank received authorization from German regulator BaFin under the EU’s Markets in Crypto-Assets regulation, or MiCA, which requires crypto service providers to be licensed. The bank cautioned that its custody service doesn’t protect clients against swings in the value of digital assets, fraud or failures by other market participants.

The custody launch isn’t Deutsche Bank’s only crypto bet. The bank is among a group of global lenders, including Goldman Sachs and Citi, backing a $270 million challenge to crypto’s dominant stablecoin issuers.

#5128. Circle hands USDC reserves to EU regulators risking higher fees

Circle’s EU-issued USDC moves under EBA supervision, potentially raising capital, liquidity and compliance requirements.

Nov. 12 marks the transfer of supervision of EU-issued USDC’s financial safeguards to the European Banking Authority, according to Circle’s quarterly filing.

Under the disclosed arrangements, the European regulator oversees reserves and redemption planning, while France’s financial regulator keeps governance responsibilities.

For Circle’s European issuer, the change could mean more capital or liquidity held in the EU and higher compliance costs. Circle says it is evaluating the impact, without putting a figure on those potential obligations in the disclosure.

The transfer follows the EBA’s Oct. 14 classification of EU-issued USDC as a significant e-money token under the Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA). Circle describes USDC as the first e-money token to receive that designation.

The handover was planned since October, establishing the token’s classification. The November handover sets the effective date for the stated division of supervisory work.

The allocation applies to USDC issued in the EU by Circle Internet Financial Europe SAS, known as Circle France.

Circle says the EBA’s direct supervisory remit covers the issuer’s own funds (regulatory capital), reserve assets, and liquidity requirements. It also oversees recovery and redemption plans.

France’s Autorité de Contrôle Prudentiel et de Résolution (ACPR) remains responsible for governance, qualifying holdings, fit-and-proper assessments, outsourcing, and conflicts of interest. Circle continues to face national oversight in those areas alongside the EBA’s financial-safeguard remit.

The memorandum covers cooperation and information exchange, as well as the allocation of supervisory responsibilities for the EU issuer.

Circle says the significant-token classification may bring increased regulatory capital, reserve, liquidity and other prudential requirements under MiCA. These could require additional capital or liquidity in the EU, raise compliance costs or adversely affect its business and financial results.

The disclosure provides no estimate of possible additional capital, liquidity, or compliance spending, and identifies no quantified financial charge arising on Nov. 12. Whether additional commitments will be needed, and their potential size, remain unresolved in that assessment.

Supervision of redemption plans concerns the issuer’s safeguards, and the note announces no revised cash-out terms or new holder fees. The disclosure identifies the responsible supervisors but does not specify any change to holders’ redemption arrangements.

#5126. Israel Discount Bank to Let Customers Trade Bitcoin in App Starting Nov. 26

Israel Discount Bank said Wednesday that from Nov. 26 it will let eligible customers buy, hold and sell bitcoin and ether in the bank’s mobile app, a launch that would beat larger rival Bank Leumi if it stays on schedule.

The Tel Aviv-listed lender described the service as a first for Israel’s banking system in a Nov. 11 statement. Customers will be able to trade the two assets alongside stocks and bonds, without opening an account at an outside exchange or managing a separate digital wallet.

The offer is limited to private, not corporate, customers who are Israeli residents, 18 or older, and whose accounts are already set up for securities trading, subject to the bank’s terms.

“For the first time in the banking system, Discount Bank will allow trading in digital currencies directly from the bank account in a simple and secure manner, without the need for complex dedicated digital wallets,” Avi Levi, CEO of Discount Group, said in the statement. “This is a first step toward establishing advanced technological infrastructure in the digital currency space and developing the bank’s capabilities in this area.”

The rollout was led by Elad Fisher, head of the bank’s financial markets division. Coins will sit in bank-provided wallets built on infrastructure from Fireblocks.

JUST IN: 🇮🇱 $150 billion Israel Discount Bank will become the 1st bank in Israel to let customers buy, hold and sell Bitcoin 👀Adoption! 👏 pic.twitter.com/gwKBy17cSf

The bank’s product page lists a 2% commission on purchases and sales in the app, not including any foreign-exchange conversion. Custody fees are waived for six months from the Nov. 26 launch and then run 0.25% a quarter. Trading is open Sunday through Friday from 9 a.m. to 11 p.m. Israel time, and only immediate orders are allowed. Customers can buy as little as 0.01 of a coin. Holdings appear in the securities portfolio at once, with cash settling the next business day in U.S. dollars; conversion into shekels happens only if the customer asks.

The service is a closed system for now. Bitcoin and ether bought through Discount cannot be sent to an outside wallet, and coins held elsewhere cannot be transferred in. The bank said it may revisit transfers later. It also warns of unusual risk, high volatility and the possibility of a total loss, and says the coins are not legal tender and lack the same protections as securities.

What Discount is selling, besides convenience, is tax treatment. Capital gains and losses on the coins can be offset automatically against the rest of the customer’s investment portfolio for tax withheld at source, the bank said, at a 25% rate on profits. Sale proceeds land directly in the customer’s bank account, without the source-of-funds questionnaires that have often accompanied money coming back from crypto platforms.

Discount is not the first Israeli bank to promise bitcoin trading. Bank Leumi, the country’s largest lender, announced a plan as early as March 2022 to offer bitcoin and ether through Pepper Invest with Paxos, a project that did not reach a full-scale launch. In August, Leumi and Galaxy Digital (GLXY) said Leumi and Pepper customers would be able to buy, hold and sell bitcoin, ether and Solana in the Leumi Trade app, with a launch expected in early 2027.

That announcement billed Leumi as the first bank in Israel to offer the service. If Discount opens on Nov. 26, it would get there first, though Leumi still plans to offer Solana, which Discount is not including at the start. Discount said it may later consider adding other assets.

In an Oct. 7 circular, the Bank of Israel’s Banking Supervision Department amended Directive 411 to say that a bank may not refuse payment services solely because the funds come from virtual-currency activity, a change that takes effect May 1, 2027. The amendment does not authorize banks to offer crypto trading, but it is part of a broader effort, first floated in a July draft, to widen how banks handle crypto-related payments.

The test now is whether Discount hits the Nov. 26 date, and whether customers accept a closed wallet and a 2% commission in exchange for bank custody and automatic tax withholding. Leumi still has early 2027 to catch up.

#5124. Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether's Tiny AI Agent Beats Models 2.5 Times Its Size at Controlling Your Phone

Tether, the company behind the world's largest stablecoin, USDT, is getting surprisingly good at making tiny AI models punch above their weight.

The company on Wednesday unveiled QVAC BotPsy, an 800-million-parameter AI agent designed to carry out everyday tasks on personal devices. Parameters are essentially the knobs that determine how much an AI model can do, and more of them generally translate to improved performance. And for reference, frontier AI models today run in the trillions of parameters.

In the company's own testing, Tether’s relatively tiny model outperformed competitors with up to 2.5 times as many parameters, suggesting that AI assistants may not need enormous models to get things done.

Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, BotPsy is agentic, meaning it’s designed to take action. It can identify which tools an application needs, execute commands in the right order, and adjust when something goes wrong.

For example, Tether described a benchmark scenario in which an AI assistant needed to send a message but encountered two problems: Wi-Fi was disabled and the inbox was full. BotPsy turned on Wi-Fi, cleared enough storage, and completed the task, while the underlying model repeatedly attempted the same failed operation.

That's a fairly mundane achievement, but it's precisely the kind of problem that makes AI agents useful outside a chatbot window.

BotPsy is built on Alibaba's Qwen3.5-0.8B architecture, a relatively small language model that Tether trained further to specialize in interacting with software tools. Its 800 million parameters represent the numerical values the model learned during training, and a smaller parameter count generally means less memory is needed to run it.

According to Tether's technical documentation, BotPsy scored 44.0% on BFCL v4, a benchmark that evaluates an AI system's ability to select and execute software functions across different tasks and conversations. Scores reflect a weighted percentage of successful performance across tool-use categories.

The underlying Qwen3.5-0.8B model scored 23.4%, while the considerably larger Qwen3.5-2B reached 37.7%.

BotPsy also scored 58.7% on ACEBench, which evaluates the accuracy of function calls, including selecting the appropriate tools and arguments. Its base model managed 30.1%, while the 2-billion-parameter competitor scored 55.4%.

The company tested five models across six tool-use evaluations, with BotPsy posting the highest results on five. Its average score across those tests reached 45.0%, compared with 38.8% for the larger Qwen model.

BotPsy is the latest addition to QVAC, Tether's effort to build an AI ecosystem that runs on devices controlled by users rather than relying entirely on centralized cloud infrastructure.

The company first introduced QVAC in May 2025, positioning the project as an alternative to AI services operated by technology giants. Its longer-term ambitions included autonomous agents capable of interacting with applications and potentially making payments using Bitcoin and USDT.

Tether expanded that initiative in October 2025 with a 41-billion-token dataset for training science and engineering models, alongside QVAC Workbench, an application that lets users run AI models locally.

Earlier this year, Tether released its SDK, providing developers with tools to build offline AI applications across mobile and desktop operating systems.

The company also developed QVAC MedPsy, a medical AI system designed to run on consumer hardware. Its 1.7-billion-parameter version outperformed Google's much larger MedGemma-27B on HealthBench Hard, a test of realistic clinical conversations evaluated using physician-developed criteria.

BotPsy is currently available as an open-weight model under the Apache 2.0 license, meaning developers can download, modify, and integrate it into their own applications. Tether has also released compressed versions intended to reduce memory requirements for local deployment.

The company has not announced a release date for a finished consumer assistant powered by BotPsy. Its published model card also says the training code and training data are not included in the release.

#5120. Binance Says It Has Fully Covered the $6.34 Billion Hack With Company Funds

Binance said Thursday it has completed coverage of the $6.34 billion Oct. 29 hack from company funds, two weeks after the largest crypto theft on record, even as withdrawals continue to reopen in phases.

In a Thursday announcement, the exchange wrote that it “has completed coverage of assets impacted in the security incident” and published a new proof-of-reserves snapshot. It repeated the same message on X.

That wording matters. Coverage means Binance has replaced the affected tokens from its own books, not that it has recovered the stolen coins from the attackers. The exchange said it committed more than $6.34 billion in company funds and bought the affected tokens through market and over-the-counter channels, drawing on a partial deployment of its Secure Asset Fund for Users (SAFU), a portion of its futures insurance fund above the level required under its risk-management framework, and its corporate treasury.

Binance has also committed to replenishing SAFU to about $1 billion after that partial draw, a step it said would keep the fund fully resourced as an emergency user-protection reserve.

A very small number of tokens the exchange no longer lists were also affected. Binance said those holdings will be handled under its delisting process and that users who hold them will be covered.

Binance took a new proof-of-reserves snapshot at 00:00 UTC on Nov. 12, or 7 p.m. Eastern on Wednesday, covering 198 tokens. That is a sharp jump from the 10 tokens included in the Oct. 31 snapshot published in the days after the breach. The new figures are now posted on the exchange’s proof-of-reserves website.

The snapshot, Binance said, “provides an updated view of assets that are fully accounted for.” Users cannot yet check that claim themselves. The exchange said the full zero-knowledge proof will take about 40 to 50 hours to generate because of the computational workload. After that, users holding the affected tokens “will be able to independently verify that their assets are backed at a minimum of 1:1.”

Until the proof is ready, the 1:1 backing is Binance’s statement, not something customers can confirm on their own.

Thursday’s update does not reopen the platform all at once. Binance said it continues to reopen withdrawals in phases and will share further details once they are confirmed. The exchange first reopened withdrawals on Nov. 1, starting with bitcoin and then expanding to other networks, and has kept the restart on a phased timetable since then.

The announcement also does not say how much of the stolen assets have been retrieved, and it does not name an attacker. Covering customer balances leaves that recovery question unanswered. The theft, which Binance first detailed in a Nov. 3 incident report, began with a fake job interview and a poisoned developer tool and drained hot wallets on Oct. 29, in what remains the largest crypto hack on record.

The exchange had already pledged more than $6.34 billion to cover customer losses in the hours after the breach, reported coverage of 90% and then 95% as it reopened withdrawals, and later said it had replenished 98% of affected tokens, with the remainder in less liquid assets that took longer to buy. Thursday’s statement is the first time Binance has said that process is complete.

Binance also said security remains a priority and that work on its infrastructure is still underway. For customers, the immediate tests are simpler: whether the zero-knowledge proof, due in about two days, backs the new snapshot, and how quickly the rest of the withdrawal queues come back.

#5119. This New AI Music Model Is Already Better Than Suno—We Put ACE to the Test

This New AI Music Model Is Already Better Than Suno—We Put ACE to the Test

Artificial intelligence has gotten pretty good at writing songs. Now, a relatively unfamiliar competitor has managed to beat Suno, arguably the biggest name in AI music, at its own game.

ACE Music v1, developed by ACE Studio, has taken first place on Artificial Analysis' vocal music leaderboard, scoring 1,160 Elo points against Suno v6's 1,144.

The AA-Music-Vocal v1.1 benchmark measures the quality of AI-generated songs containing vocals. Evaluators listen to tracks produced from identical prompts without knowing which model created them, then choose their favorite. Those preferences determine an Elo-style rating, with higher scores indicating stronger listener preference.

The lead is relatively narrow, and the models' statistical confidence intervals overlap. Suno also retains the top position on the separate instrumental music leaderboard.

ACE Studio, founded in 2018, originally focused on synthesizing singing voices from MIDI notes and lyrics. Its latest ACE Music v1 model generates complete songs from written descriptions or user-provided lyrics.

In Decrypt's hands-on tests, ACE Music consistently produced more melodic and coherent songs in mainstream styles.

That said, the vocal performances sounded more natural in general, with fewer of the synthetic artifacts that sometimes make AI singers sound like they're struggling to pronounce their own lyrics, use a lot of autotune or hallucinate words just to fill spaces. (Go ahead and give our song a listen here, which incidentally is about our latest company announcement regarding the Information Exchange.)

ACE also handled song endings better. Where Suno occasionally introduces strange sounds, unnecessary repetitions, hallucinations or abrupt musical changes near the finish, ACE generally maintained its structure and delivered cleaner conclusions.

That matters because making a convincing chorus is relatively easy compared with maintaining a musical idea throughout an entire composition even if the lyrics are already over.

Suno still had an advantage when we pushed the models toward less conventional genres.

In our melodic death metal test, ACE consistently produced tracks featuring a woman singing clearly in a pleasant, clean voice, missing the aggressive vocal delivery expected from the requested style. In general it confuses metal genres all over. Suno offered more creative arrangements in the proper heavy styles, with better stylistic consistency in several similarly demanding prompts.

The results were subjective rather than a controlled benchmark, but they showed why a leaderboard victory doesn't automatically make one model better for every musician.

ACE's most interesting feature may be its songwriting workflow.

Its “Idea” feature can develop a basic concept into lyrics and a structured composition, identifying individual song sections and describing how they should be arranged. Users can then refine the material rather than repeatedly generating completely new tracks.

The v1.1 Preview goes further, creating a musical score before generating audio.

“Each generation is a new performance, so the sound may differ even when the score stays the same,” the developers explain.

Users can edit melodies and chords, regenerate performances, export MIDI files and separate vocals from instruments for further production.

That workflow puts ACE closer to a music production assistant than a simple prompt-to-song application.

The flagship ACE Music v1 model remains closed-source with no public API, so users can only generate via the official site. However, its developers are pursuing a parallel open-source strategy.

ACE Studio and StepFun have already released ACE-Step 1.5, a separate family of models whose code and weights are publicly available. Unlike the proprietary flagship, these can run on users' own hardware—but using a weaker model.

The company also promises another release.

“We plan to open-source ACE Music v1 Flash in late 2026,” the developers state on their website, describing a version designed to balance quality, generation speed and hardware requirements.

ACE Music is available through its browser application, with a limited free tier and paid subscriptions. Commercial usage rights for flagship-model songs require a paid plan, while ACE-Step offers a separate free generation option.

For now, ACE Music v1 holds a 16-point Elo lead over Suno v6 in vocal generation. Its promised Flash release would give local users another model to try before the end of 2026.

#5115. Simon Dixon: What It Would Take for Me to Sell My Bitcoin for Gold

Simon Dixon, CEO and co-founder of BnkToTheFuture, tells BMTV there are only three things that would make him sell all of his Bitcoin and buy gold.

Simon Dixon, CEO and co-founder of BnkToTheFuture, tells BMTV there are only three things that would make him sell all of his Bitcoin and buy gold. Since 2011, he says Bitcoin has done three things incredibly well: you can earn and hold it in self-custody, spend it peer-to-peer without a government in the middle, and rely on a fixed supply of 21 million as a hedge against long-term debasement. If any of those three is ever called into question, he would sell immediately. The real battle, in his view, is not whether Bitcoin gets adopted — he says institutional adoption is on a trajectory we have never seen — but whether custodians, Wall Street-owned miners, and treasury companies end up centralizing the network.

#5111. Bitcoin Short-Term Holders Send $3.6 Billion in BTC to Exchanges at a Loss

Bitcoin Short-Term Holders Send $3.6 Billion in BTC to Exchanges at a Loss

Bitcoin investors who bought recently are moving tens of thousands of coins to cryptocurrency exchanges at a loss, adding to concerns about selling pressure as the cryptocurrency falls toward the low-$70,000 range.

CryptoQuant’s latest short-term holder profit-and-loss chart shows roughly 47,300 BTC transferred to exchanges below their acquisition price over 24 hours, compared with only about 1,100 BTC arriving at a profit.

Combined inflows stood near 48,500 BTC, or around $3.6 billion at Bitcoin’s current price of approximately $74,000.

The research company's chart below shows transfers, not completed sales. Sending Bitcoin to an exchange can be the first step toward selling, but some deposits are made for trading for other tokens, collateral, or other services. The data also does not establish exactly how much of the transferred Bitcoin was subsequently sold.

The distinction is important because the chart measures the behavior of short-term holders, a group that CryptoQuant generally defines as investors holding Bitcoin for less than 155 days.

These are often the buyers most exposed to a sudden price reversal, particularly when their holdings are worth less than they paid.

In a November 11 CryptoQuant analysis, contributor Amr Taha identified two large waves of underwater exchange deposits over the previous week: 50,790 BTC on November 3 and 46,060 BTC on November 10.

“On November 10, profitable transfers stood near 1,700 BTC, meaning approximately 27 BTC arrived at a loss for every 1 BTC transferred at a profit,” Taha wrote.

That is an unusually one-sided flow. Investors moving coins into exchanges are overwhelmingly doing so at prices below those at which they acquired them, rather than cashing in gains from the earlier rally.

A similar pattern has occurred over several months, with recurring spikes in loss-making exchange transfers as Bitcoin’s price swung higher and lower. The latest red spike appears near the deepest readings visible since July.

A separate CryptoQuant chart breaks down deposits to Binance by the time since each Bitcoin last moved on-chain. It shows 3,290 BTC arriving from coins last moved one day to one week earlier on November 1, followed by a much larger 8,114 BTC from the same category on November 3.

On November 9, the platform also received 1,925 BTC from coins last moved one to three months earlier, according to Taha’s analysis. Those figures help identify which age groups are active, but they do not necessarily reveal when the owners originally purchased their Bitcoin.

The Binance chart measures one exchange and groups coins by their last on-chain movement. The profit-and-loss charts track short-term holders sending coins to exchanges more broadly.

The timing matters because Bitcoin has been sliding from its late-September highs above $85,000 into the $73,000-to-$74,000 area visible at the end of the screenshots. A newer buyer who paid $80,000 would be sitting on a paper loss of roughly 7.5% with Bitcoin at $74,000; selling at that price would lock it in.

The recent weakness is not limited to short-term holders. Glassnode identified about 4 million BTC held by longer-term investors that had been acquired between $85,000 and $118,000, potentially leaving another pocket of underwater supply above current prices.

BitGo BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) has added USDC (USDC) minting and redemption to its Go Accounts, the digital asset infrastructure firm said Thursday.

The service is available to eligible customers of BitGo Bank & Trust, its digital asset trust bank subsidiary regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

Clients can initiate and monitor their USDC orders on the BitGo platform or through its API, according to a statement shared with The Block. BitGo will route them through its Circle Mint account, charging flat fees that clients see before they confirm the transaction.

However, clients funding or withdrawing fiat will still have to work within the applicable banking processing windows and settlement times.

#5108. Peter Schiff on the Bitcoin Crash: “Get the Hell Out”

Challenged on why two or three down years would matter for Bitcoin when gold and the Nasdaq have had similar streaks before recoveries, Peter Schiff says the difference is the backdrop.

Challenged on why two or three down years would matter for Bitcoin when gold and the Nasdaq have had similar streaks before recoveries, Peter Schiff says the difference is the backdrop. When a market can’t go up on the best news it has ever had, he argues, that normally indicates that it’s going to go down. He calls Bitcoin “all hype,” with no intrinsic value, income, or dividends, and says much of the supply now sits with ETF investors and Bitcoin treasury companies rather than people who want to use it as money. All of that hot money, he warns, is going to exit, the market will go down faster than it went up, and he expects a massive washout. Even long-term bulls, he says, will have cheaper opportunities after leveraged speculators are cleared out, and he adds that this is not the time to be hodling.

#5105. Stablecoin Drain, ETF Outflows Leave Bitcoin 'Running on Empty'

Stablecoin Drain, ETF Outflows Leave Bitcoin 'Running on Empty'

The pool of dollars that fuels crypto trading is draining.

Total stablecoin supply fell by $14.2 billion over the 30 days through Nov. 9, the fastest contraction since June 2022, according to a report published Wednesday by onchain analytics firm Glassnode.

The total stablecoin supply now sits just below $280 billion, and the research company suggests that's a big reason why Bitcoin is currently "running on empty."

Stablecoins, tokens typically pegged to the U.S. dollar, serve as crypto’s cash. Traders park money in them on exchanges and onchain before buying other assets.

When the stablecoin supply shrinks, it means holders are redeeming tokens for dollars and pulling that money out of crypto entirely, which Glassnode said leaves “less fuel for a rally.” The firm said a return to supply growth would be the first sign that fresh money is coming in.

The stablecoin drain isn’t the only sign of money leaving. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs saw about $1.6 billion in net redemptions over 20 trading sessions, the report found, and Bitcoin has traded at a discount on Coinbase, a gauge of U.S. demand.

Bitcoin also slipped below the average cost basis of short-term holders, about $75,600, leaving most recent buyers underwater, and traded near $73,000 on Wednesday. Over $3.6 billion in Bitcoin was recently moved over to exchanges, potentially to be sold, at a loss, according to a separate report from CryptoQuant today.

Glassnode did point to some silver linings. The slide has flushed out most leveraged long positions near the current price, buy orders outweigh sell orders in the order book, and wallets holding between 100 and 10,000 BTC have started accumulating.

The firm flagged $73,000 as key support and said the broader trend change that began in August remains intact as long as Bitcoin holds above its June low.

The contraction comes as stablecoins face growing competition from traditional finance.

Global banks including Goldman Sachs, Citi, and Deutsche Bank are backing a $270 million challenge to crypto’s dominant stablecoin issuers, and the Federal Reserve recently unveiled stablecoin rules on reserves and capital.

#5100. Nvidia-Backed CoreWeave Is Surging: Here’s What It Means for the AI Boom

Nvidia-Backed CoreWeave Is Surging: Here’s What It Means for the AI Boom

Nvidia-backed CoreWeave soared 24.7% Wednesday, helping revive the AI trade after weeks of selling, as the company reported surging demand for the expensive computing hardware powering the artificial intelligence boom.

The cloud provider posted $3.66 billion in third-quarter revenue, up 168% from a year ago, and raised its 2026 sales forecast to $13 billion to $13.3 billion. The results also helped lift Nvidia, along with AI infrastructure stocks including Nebius, IREN, Oracle and Applied Digital.

For investors watching Nvidia, CoreWeave offers a useful look at what happens after the chipmaker sells its processors.

CoreWeave buys Nvidia GPUs, installs them in data centers, and rents that computing power to companies developing or running AI models, including OpenAI and other major customers.

That makes its revenue growth evidence that businesses are still willing to pay for AI computing capacity. It does not, by itself, establish that the enormous spending required to supply that capacity will produce sustainable profits.

Nvidia's connection goes well beyond a typical supplier relationship. In January, Nvidia invested another $2 billion in CoreWeave shares at $87.20 each, expanding a business arrangement aimed at building more than five gigawatts of AI data-center capacity by 2030.

The companies also agreed to introduce new Nvidia technology across CoreWeave's infrastructure, including its Rubin platform and Vera CPUs. That makes CoreWeave an important showcase and customer for future Nvidia hardware, not just the chips already installed.

“Together, we’re racing to meet extraordinary demand for NVIDIA AI factories,” Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said when the expanded deal was announced in January.

Nvidia also has a $6.3 billion initial agreement to buy CoreWeave's unused computing capacity through April 2032, subject to the contract's conditions. In practice, the arrangement gives CoreWeave a buyer for capacity it cannot sell elsewhere, while Nvidia gets access to cloud computing built around its own chips.

The relationship explains both the excitement and some of the criticism surrounding AI investment. Nvidia sells the hardware, invests in a company buying that hardware, and has also committed to purchasing some of the computing services it helps create. Inventors call it “circular finance.” AI bros call it a meme. Skeptics call it a warning sign.

Nvidia gives money to OpenAI who gives money to Coreweave who gives money to Nvidia who gives money to OpenAi who gives money to Coreweave who gives money to OpenAi who gives money to Nvidia pic.twitter.com/3UFfK7KwJv

—  Q-Cap  (@qcapital2020) September 22, 2025

Those are real commercial agreements, but the overlapping financial relationships complicate attempts to measure independent demand.

CoreWeave reported $132.5 billion in revenue backlog, meaning contracted business that it expects to deliver over time, not money already in the bank. The company says it is working with customers including OpenAI, Cohere, and Perplexity, and had more than 1.7 gigawatts of active power capacity at the end of September.

“This was a breakout quarter for CoreWeave, with revenue increasing by more than $1 billion sequentially,” CEO Michael Intrator said in the company's earnings release.

The company also expects about $9 billion in customer prepayments and deposits over the next three months. Getting paid before delivering all the contracted services could reduce its immediate need to borrow at a time when investors have become increasingly uncomfortable with the debt financing AI data centers.

The market reaction extended beyond CoreWeave. Wednesday's rebound touched Nvidia shares and the broader technology market, with the Nasdaq gaining 1.6%. CoreWeave's report arrived as investors were trying to decide whether recent AI-stock losses reflected weakening demand or anxiety about the cost of meeting it.

These specialized GPU-rental businesses are known as neoclouds. Some—including CoreWeave, which began as an Ethereum miner—have roots in crypto infrastructure, where operators were already accustomed to running large amounts of power-hungry hardware.

Bernstein analysts described the quarter as “about the best quarter we could have asked for,” according to Reuters, but warned CoreWeave would be among the companies hit hardest if the shortage of available AI computing capacity begins to ease.

The Nvidia partnership does not remove that risk. Newer processors can make older GPUs less competitive, while electricity availability, construction delays, and higher borrowing costs can all squeeze returns. An oversupply of AI computing would also put pressure on the rental prices CoreWeave earns.

#5098. SoFiUSD goes live on Polygon as bank-issued stablecoin expands reach

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The bank-issued stablecoin joins USDC, USDT, and PYUSD on Polygon Open Money Stack.

SoFiUSD is now live on Polygon, giving SoFi’s bank-issued stablecoin another blockchain for payments and settlement as the fintech expands its digital asset business.

In a statement shared with The Block on Thursday, Polygon Labs said the stablecoin is available on Polygon Open Money Stack, adding to existing Ethereum and Solana support. Polygon Labs said SoFi’s more than 17 million members can buy, sell, hold, and convert SoFiUSD on Polygon through the SoFi app.

SoFiUSD is issued by SoFi Bank, N.A., an OCC-regulated national bank, and is redeemable one-to-one for U.S. dollars. It joins Circle's USDC, Tether's USDT, and PayPal's PYUSD on Polygon Open Money Stack.

According to the statement, the integration gives SoFiUSD access to wallets, fiat rails, stablecoin conversion, and cross-chain settlement on a single interface, allowing customers to use the asset without the need for a separate wallet or manual bridging.

“SoFi is well positioned to bring stablecoins into mainstream finance because it combines the regulatory foundation of a national bank with the reach of a major consumer and enterprise technology platform,” Polygon Labs CEO Marc Boiron said.

The development is the latest in a series of crypto initiatives from SoFi this year. Last month, the company reported 503,000 crypto products at the end of the third quarter, while gross crypto transaction revenue reached $161.6 million, up 20% from the prior quarter.

SoFi also launched its tokenized deposit product in October, allowing customers to convert deposits into SoFiUSD at a one-to-one rate. In September, the bank began settling card transactions using its stablecoin on Mastercard’s network, weeks after agreeing to connect its SoFi Exchange Network with Kraken’s infrastructure.

#5095. US data center restrictions could give Bitcoin miners an edge in AI, though challenges remain

As US voters push back against new data centers, Bitcoin miners already expanding into AI could benefit from their existing facilities.

Bitcoin miners with power already secured could have an edge in the race to build AI data centers after voters in several US communities backed limits on new development. The midterms could also bring closer scrutiny from Congress over who pays for the grid upgrades those facilities need, industry experts told Cointelegraph.

Voters in several states weighed in on data center development during the Nov. 3 US midterms, when local measures appeared on the same ballots as congressional races. Amid concerns about rising electricity costs, water use and the impact on nearby communities, voters favored limiting development in 40 of the 41 local contests tracked by Ballotpedia.

The restrictions include citywide bans, limits on larger facilities, temporary pauses on new projects and voter approval requirements for some developments.

The results come as major Bitcoin mining companies continue moving into AI, a transition Jamie Brown, CEO of Global Kiwi Labs, said could become more attractive as local restrictions make it harder to build new data centers.

“If a Bitcoin miner has not yet repurposed their electric contract and physical plant to AI, the case to do so just got more compelling,” said Brown, whose company hosts servers for crypto exchanges and trading firms.

Related: US midterm elections 2026 as it happened: Democrats win control of Congress

Miners’ shift to AI infrastructure is already underway, with publicly listed Bitcoin miners announcing more than $70 billion in AI and advanced computing contracts during 2025 and early 2026, according to a first-quarter report by CoinShares.

But state and local decisions can have a more immediate impact on whether data center projects move forward. That’s because local authorities often oversee zoning and building permits and can influence access to electricity.

“A company might have access to an attractive site and a significant amount of power capacity, but if local restrictions prevent it from expanding or upgrading that infrastructure, the opportunity becomes much more difficult to pursue,” said Jordan Knecht, head of strategic integrations at Kresus, a blockchain infrastructure company.

Last week, voters backed 27 measures to limit or oppose new data centers in the elections, according to Ballotpedia. These included five complete bans, 12 partial bans and six-month pauses on new projects in three Maine towns.

In Grove City, Ohio, voters approved a rule requiring public approval for large projects that use more than 20 megawatts of electricity, among other conditions.

“Federal policy will certainly influence the broader investment environment, but the practical ability to develop and operate these facilities often comes down to conditions in individual markets,” Knecht told Cointelegraph. “For miners looking to transition into AI, I think understanding those local dynamics will be just as important as following developments in Washington.”

Building AI-ready data center infrastructure can cost between $8 million and $15 million per megawatt, compared with $700,000 to $1 million per megawatt for traditional mining facilities, according to a 2026 report by CoinShares.

That gap helps explain why a site with power already secured is only a starting point for miners moving into AI. “Transitioning from Bitcoin mining to AI workloads requires significant additional investment in infrastructure for cooling, networking, and reliability,” Knecht said.

Yuliya Barabash, founder of SBSB Fintech Lawyers, also pointed to differences in electricity needs between Bitcoin mining and AI data centers. Bitcoin miners can often reduce electricity use when prices rise, while AI data centers typically need a consistent power supply, she said.

“Higher electricity prices and additional regulatory requirements could make some projects less profitable or delay their implementation,” Barabash told Cointelegraph.

This comes as electricity demand from US data centers is also expected to rise, according to the International Energy Agency (IEA). The agency estimates that data centers used 224 terawatt-hours of electricity in 2025, a figure projected to nearly double to 426 terawatt-hours by 2030.

One question facing the incoming Democratic-controlled Congress is who should pay to expand the grid for large data centers.

Knecht said lawmakers could take a closer look at how those projects affect electricity costs and how they get approved. “There may be more pressure to ensure that large data center operators are paying their fair share of infrastructure costs rather than passing those expenses on to consumers.”

Barabash also expects scrutiny of who pays for the electricity infrastructure needed to support data centers. “One of the key priorities is likely to be ensuring that technology companies, rather than ordinary consumers, bear the costs of the additional electricity infrastructure their facilities require,” she said.

Still, experts noted that closer attention does not necessarily mean Congress will discourage data center development. “AI infrastructure is becoming increasingly important to the US economy, and there is still a strong incentive to support investment in domestic computing capacity,” Knecht said.

For miners weighing a conversion, the question is whether AI revenue can justify the cost of upgrading cooling, networking and power systems. Brown thinks it can. “Miners will swap to AI because it is a better ROI than mining,” he said.

#5093. Pressure Mounts on Nvidia's Jensen Huang Amid Reports of Second Affair

Pressure Mounts on Nvidia's Jensen Huang Amid Reports of Second Affair

Nvidia's board is preparing to remove CEO Jensen Huang after reports that he had two extramarital affairs, one with a woman U.S. intelligence believes is a Chinese spy and another with a company executive, The Wall Street Journal reported on Wednesday.

The board was planning to meet as soon as Wednesday to determine Huang's future, according to people familiar with the matter cited by the Journal. It was not immediately clear who would succeed him, the paper said.

The New York Times reported on Wednesday that Huang had an extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of health care, who reports to him, citing five people who worked at the company. The Journal also reported the relationship, citing a person familiar with the matter.

Nvidia's code of conduct tells employees to avoid situations that create conflicts, including personal relationships, according to the Times. When the newspaper reached him by phone, Huang said, "I don't think you should be talking to me directly," before hanging up. Nvidia declined to comment, and Powell did not respond to requests for comment from either paper.

It is the second affair involving Huang to come to light. The New York Times reported in October that U.S. officials had concluded Alice Huang, an executive at Singapore-based Nvidia customer Megaspeed, was a Chinese intelligence asset who had developed a close relationship with Jensen Huang. The Financial Times reported last week that a U.S. government memo said the two had been romantically involved. China has denied the allegations.

On Sunday, Huang acknowledged in a letter to staff that he had been unfaithful to his wife, without naming the person involved. He had told Nvidia's board that he had been intimate with Alice Huang, the Times reported, prompting the board to open an independent investigation, led by law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz.

The Justice Department is now leading multiple investigations involving Alice Huang, the Journal reported. Huang also faces a proposed shareholder class action and the threat of a congressional subpoena.

Nvidia shares were down 5.9% at $163.43 in premarket trading early on Thursday, according to Yahoo! Finance data, after closing up 2.9% at $173.73 on Wednesday, before the reports emerged. The stock had already lost about $430 billion in market value on Monday and Tuesday.

Traders expect further losses. On Decrypt Predict, users put an 89% chance on Nvidia trading at or below $160 during regular market hours at some point in November, according to the market. By contrast, they gave just a 6% chance that the stock would climb back to $216 this month.

Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and is its largest shareholder, owning about 3.6% of the company. He has said he does not believe in succession planning, according to the Journal, and the company's latest proxy statement warns that it is "highly dependent" on its longstanding executive team, raising questions over who could replace him.

Potential successors are reportedly under discussion already. One person inside the company told the Financial Times that the simplest option would be to appoint chief financial officer Colette Kress as interim CEO, while two sources pointed to Ian Buck, who runs Nvidia's data center business. Analysts cited Arm CEO Rene Haas, a former Nvidia executive, as a possible outside candidate. "We always knew key man risk was an issue for Nvidia," Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon told the paper.

At least one high-profile figure has offered Huang a way forward. Responding to a tweet urging him to hire Huang, Tesla CEO Elon Musk wrote: "That would be an honor."

#5092. Securitize and Ethena’s $100 million BlackRock BUIDL liquidity facility goes live

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The launch follows an earlier integration between Ethena Labs and Securitize to offer 24/7 atomic swaps between BlackRock's tokenized fund and Ethena's stablecoin.

Securitize and Ethena Labs have launched a $100 million, 24/7 liquidity facility for BlackRock's USD Institutional Digital Liquidity Fund (BUIDL). It will allow eligible investors to exchange shares of the tokenized fund for supported stablecoins, including USDC, outside traditional market hours.

The facility launches with $50 million in initial liquidity, split evenly between BUIDL and USDC, with another $50 million available within an hour as liquidity is used.

The initiative builds on the companies' existing collaboration around BUIDL and USDtb, Ethena's digital dollar product backed primarily by BUIDL. The firms first announced the $100 million facility in June.

"With this facility now live, we're taking another step toward a financial system where institutional investors can access liquidity when they need it, not just when traditional markets are open," said Carlos Domingo, co-founder and CEO of Securitize. "That's the infrastructure we believe will help bring more of the world's assets onchain."

The launch follows an earlier integration between Ethena Labs and Securitize in June 2025 to offer 24/7 atomic swaps between BlackRock's tokenized fund and Ethena's stablecoin.

"By bringing this facility online with Securitize, we're helping close the gap between traditional market hours and the always-on nature of blockchain markets," said Guy Young, founder of Ethena Labs. "It's another important step in making tokenized assets more useful for institutional participants."

The latest development also comes as BlackRock expands BUIDL's presence in onchain markets. In February, BlackRock and Securitize tapped Uniswap for direct onchain BUIDL trading, allowing eligible investors to trade the $2.4 billion tokenized Treasury fund through UniswapX.

#5090. DOJ’s Operation TorNado Arrests 354 and Seizes $234 Million in Cash and Crypto

The Justice Department and Europol said Thursday that Operation TorNado has produced 354 arrests and the seizure of more than $234 million in cash and virtual currencies, in what the FBI called the largest takedown yet of dark web drug vendors and buyers.

The announcement came from the department’s Joint Criminal Opioid and Darknet Enforcement team, or JCODE, an FBI-led group created in 2018. Officials said Operation TorNado is the sixth, and most extensive, internationally coordinated action since then, and that it builds on last year’s Operation RapTor. The sweep ran from May 2025 to October 2026.

Europol, which coordinated work with agencies in 14 countries, published a parallel statement the same day. The operation targeted vendors and buyers who had been active on five dark web markets already seized by law enforcement: Archetyp, Bohemia, Nemesis, Kingdom and Tor2Door. Officials also identified accounts on DrugHub and on forums, single-vendor shops and encrypted messaging services.

The seizures included about 5.7 metric tons of illicit drugs worldwide, among them fentanyl and other opioids, methamphetamine, heroin, cocaine, ecstasy and MDMA, plus 38 pill presses and 209 firearms. Neither the Justice Department nor Europol split the $234 million between cash and cryptocurrency, and neither named bitcoin or any other coin in the official totals.

Europol put the European share at more than €60 million in cash and cryptocurrencies, alongside 950 kilograms of drugs and 57 firearms, and the U.S. share at more than €150 million, or $166 million, in cash and cryptocurrencies, plus more than 4.8 metric tons of drugs and 152 firearms. Those regional money figures still combine cash and crypto. Europol said its forensic and blockchain analysis teams traced illicit transactions as part of the work.

The closest official window into how the vendors were paid comes from the cases the Justice Department highlighted and from court papers posted with the announcement.

Alexander Green, 32, of Jacksonville, North Carolina, was arrested in June and charged in the Southern District of Ohio with conspiracy to distribute controlled substances, including fentanyl. The department said he allegedly operated a vendor called STARLABS on DrugHub, which it described as a Monero-only dark web market, and that the group has been linked to two overdose deaths. A criminal complaint says undercover purchases from STARLABS were made “in exchange for cryptocurrency.” If convicted, he faces up to 20 years in prison.

In a separate Florida arrest, brothers Stanislav Chernyshov, 30, and Vladislav Chernyshov, 35, are charged in the Eastern District of Virginia with conspiracy to distribute Schedule I synthetic opioids. Prosecutors say they allegedly ran the vendor “BarbaraWhite,” selling counterfeit oxycodone pills laced with nitazenes and shipping more than 1,000 parcels across 49 states. A search-warrant affidavit describes bitcoin transfers tied to that account and the Nemesis market. They each face up to 20 years if convicted.

Operation TorNado led to 132 arrests in the United States, 59 in the United Kingdom, 45 in Hungary and 41 in Germany, along with arrests in France, New Zealand, the Netherlands, Slovenia, Austria, Ireland, Czechia, Australia and Finland. A number of investigations are still ongoing to identify the people behind remaining dark web accounts, the department said.

FBI Director Kash Patel called Operation TorNado “the largest interagency takedown of Dark Web drug vendors and buyers to date.” IRS Criminal Investigation Chief Jarod Koopman, whose agency was part of the JCODE group, put the financial work at the center: “Criminal networks may operate across borders and in the shadows of the Dark Web, but they cannot operate without money.”

Federal prosecutions are underway in more than 20 districts. An indictment or complaint only alleges that crimes were committed, and the defendants are presumed innocent until proven guilty.

What the announcement does not settle is how much of the $234 million was cryptocurrency, and how much of that was bitcoin. Officials have not said. The record on Operation TorNado shows a combined cash-and-crypto seizure, a Monero-only market in at least one featured case, and bitcoin transfers in court papers, without a breakdown of the total.

#5089. Nomura explores sale of crypto unit Laser Digital: Bloomberg

The potential sale of Laser Digital follows a fourth straight quarterly loss for Nomura’s European operations.

Japanese financial services giant Nomura Holdings is reportedly weighing a sale of its cryptocurrency subsidiary Laser Digital as Nomura’s European operations continue to post losses.

Nomura has held talks with potential buyers, including Marex Group, Bloomberg reported Wednesday. The company has yet to make a final decision and could keep the business, according to the report.

Nomura’s European businesses posted a pretax loss of 7.4 billion yen ($46.5 million) in the quarter ended Sept. 30, 2026, their fourth consecutive quarterly loss. The company posted pretax profits in every other reporting region over the same period. Nomura did not disclose whether Laser Digital, which is headquartered in Switzerland, contributed to the European result.

Nomura is one of Japan’s largest financial services groups, with its investment management division overseeing 152.8 trillion yen ($960 billion) in assets as of September 2026.

The potential sale comes less than three months after Laser Digital Japan secured the country’s first new crypto exchange license in four years, allowing the firm to expand its institutional crypto trading services in Japan.

Established in 2022, Laser Digital provides institutional cryptocurrency trading, asset management and venture investment services. The company has also pursued expansion in the United States, receiving preliminary approval from the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency for its proposed national trust bank in May.

During a January earnings call, chief financial officer Hiroyuki Moriuchi said the company would reduce its cryptocurrency trading positions to limit short-term volatility while continuing to expand Laser Digital over the medium to long term.

#5087. DTCC plans to bring tokenized securities to Solana in second half of 2027

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The planned Solana integration follows the launch of DTCC's tokenization service on Canton Network and its private Besu network last month.

The Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation announced Thursday it plans to bring tokenized versions of eligible securities held by its Depository Trust Company subsidiary to Solana Solana (SOL) in the second half of 2027, connecting its DTCC Tokenization Service to the Layer 1 blockchain.

The service went live on Oct. 28, initially on Canton Network and DTCC's private Besu network. Eligible users can convert their securities into tokenized versions and back again, with delivery to digital wallets of their choice. DTCC said at launch that it planned to add further blockchain networks next year.

"DTC-tokenized assets made available on Solana – and all blockchain networks integrated with the service – will retain the same investor protections, ownership rights and regulatory safeguards that apply to traditionally held securities," Brian Steele, president of DTCC Clearing & Securities Services, said in the announcement.

DTC received a Securities and Exchange Commission no-action letter last December for the tokenization service, allowing DTC to tokenize Russell 1000 stocks, major U.S. equity index exchange-traded funds, and U.S. Treasury bills, bonds, and notes.

DTCC's first limited production trades of tokenized stocks and Treasurys began in July on Canton and Besu, with JPMorgan tokenizing part of its Invesco QQQ Trust holdings held at DTCC in the trial. The bank retained the ability to convert them back to traditional shares.

DTCC named 20 participants that had signed agreements to use the service at its October launch, including Bank of America, Citi, JPMorgan, Morgan Stanley, and UBS. DTCC said its depository subsidiary provided custody and asset servicing for $114 trillion in securities in 2025.

#5086. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $1.4B in Five Days as Investors Cut Risk

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $1.4B in Five Days as Investors Cut Risk

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds have recorded five consecutive days of net outflows, as investors retreat from risk assets after a turbulent week for markets.

The funds lost some $1.44 billion between November 5 and November 11, including $299.9 million on Wednesday, according to SoSoValue data. That brings net outflows for November so far to $1.88 billion, after $520 million left the funds in October.

The outflows have coincided with a sell-off in AI and technology stocks. On November 5, when the Nasdaq Composite fell 5.5% in a single day, Bitcoin ETFs saw net outflows of about $430 million.

"This wave of outflows is the result of institutional investors actively or passively reducing their risk exposure following a decline in macro risk appetite, which is not yet sufficient to prove that Bitcoin's long-term allocation thesis has reversed," Tim Sun, senior researcher at HashKey, told Decrypt.

The five-day outflows amount to about 2.6% of the funds' $55.1 billion in cumulative net inflows, "far from a full-scale retreat," Sun said. But the funds' total net assets have fallen to $93.7 billion, down about 45% from a peak of $169.5 billion in October 2025, as both outflows and falling prices take their toll.

Bitcoin is trading at $73,358, down 0.87% over the past 24 hours, according to Decrypt data. On Decrypt Predict, users put a 40% chance on Bitcoin trading above $74,000 on Sunday afternoon, and a 94% chance that it will hold above $70,000. Sun estimated that the average holding cost for spot Bitcoin ETFs is around $81,000, meaning some ETF holders are now sitting on unrealized losses, and that some investors may cut their exposure further as they reassess their allocations at year-end.

Analysts say the bigger concern is a shortage of new buyers. In a note shared with Decrypt, Greg Cipolaro, head of research at BitGo, said the post-election bounce brought only $75 million into spot Bitcoin ETFs, while the supply of dollar-pegged stablecoins contracted 1.9% to $286.3 billion, "suggesting little fresh capital is waiting on the sidelines." Onchain analytics firm Glassnode found that total stablecoin supply fell by $14.2 billion in the 30 days through November 9, the fastest contraction since June 2022, Decrypt reported on Wednesday.

The basis trade, in which hedge funds bought spot ETF shares while shorting futures to capture the price gap, has also become less attractive because the basis has fallen below borrowing costs, prompting many funds to exit, Cipolaro said. That means ETF flows may now reflect directional demand for Bitcoin more directly than before, according to Sun.

Corporate buyers have also slowed. The premium of Strategy's stock over the value of its Bitcoin holdings has narrowed, making it less attractive for the company to raise capital to buy more, while MSCI's decision to exclude it from its indexes could cut off passive investment, Sun said.

"The most critical factor to watch right now is the persistent lack of buying demand itself, the impact of which on prices may be more significant than the outflow figures alone," he said.

Cipolaro said Bitcoin had fallen with risk assets but lagged their recoveries. "Unless it begins to participate in rallies and ETF demand strengthens, digital assets could remain exposed to further downside without benefiting from a broader risk asset rebound," he wrote.

Many people forget just how long the 'winter' was for $GLD. It had an initial burst, even becaming the biggest ETF in world for one day in 2011 ,and then the bad times came and and it (saw 40% of investors bail) and took nearly a DECADE to get back to its that aum high again.… pic.twitter.com/Ny9gyyo1Am

— Eric Balchunas (@EricBalchunas) November 11, 2026

Not everyone sees the outflows as a long-term warning sign. Bloomberg ETF analyst Eric Balchunas compared Bitcoin ETFs with gold ETF GLD, which saw around 40% of its investors leave after its 2011 peak and took nearly a decade to recover its assets, but now holds nearly three times that early high. "Assets without cash flows run purely on sentiment and it changes," he wrote.

#5085. Uncalled US House races could send more crypto allies to Congress

The remaining races feature pro-crypto candidates and others without a stated position, giving advocacy groups what they see as “an opportunity” for outreach.

Just over a week after a “blue wave” handed Democrats control of the US House and Senate, 14 races have yet to be called. The results so far have added crypto supporters in both chambers and the remaining races could bring more into Congress.

Three candidates in the uncalled races received backing from crypto political action committees (PACs). According to Stand With Crypto, every candidate in those races is either strongly pro-crypto or has no stated position.

As crypto advocacy groups look ahead to 2028, they’re turning their attention to candidates who have yet to take a public stance on crypto.

Crypto PACs backed three candidates in US House races that remain undecided. Two incumbents lead in Alaska and North Carolina, while a Louisiana state senator faces a December runoff.

Rep. Nick Begich leads in Alaska’s US House race. Defend American Jobs, Fairshake’s Republican-facing affiliate, backed him with $508,142 in the August primary. The same PAC put $150,600 behind Rep. David Rouzer, who leads in North Carolina’s 7th District.

Louisiana state Sen. Blake Miguez will face Democrat Chauna Banks in a Dec. 12 runoff for the state’s 6th District. The Fellowship PAC backed Miguez with $250,000.

The Associated Press called the race in South Carolina’s 2nd District for incumbent Rep. Joe Wilson on the morning of Nov. 11. Stand With Crypto rates Wilson as “strongly supportive of crypto.” His win brought the group’s House tally to 246 crypto supporters and 107 opponents, while the Senate stood at 50 supporters and 33 opponents.

In the remaining races, Stand With Crypto lists each candidate as either strongly supportive of crypto or having no stated position. “The new members that have no stance present an opportunity,” Blockchain Association Chief Policy Officer Lindsay Fraser told Cointelegraph.

Nearly one in four members of the incoming Congress will be new, Fraser said, and many have given little thought to blockchain technology. “To us, that represents a blank slate in terms of education,” she said.

Blockchain Association is planning a January fly-in to reach newly elected lawmakers. “Leaving the 120th Congress with more members in that A and B category would be a huge success,” she said, referring to Stand With Crypto’s ratings. The group gives an A to candidates who strongly support crypto and a B to those who are somewhat supportive.

Fraser said age plays a bigger role than party affiliation in how lawmakers vote on crypto. She said members under 55 are much more likely to support the industry and that the incoming class skews younger.

Fraser said she wants the industry to enter the 2028 election “with two candidates who […] are thoughtful in regards to the industry,” so the sector can avoid “living and dying by the outcome of that election.”

#5083. Jane Street Lifts Its Strategy (MSTR) Stake 54% to 4.1 Million Shares

A 13F filed Thursday shows Jane Street held 4,119,097 Strategy shares as of Sept. 30, up 53.8%.

Jane Street Group raised its Strategy (MSTR) stake 53.8% in the third quarter, to 4,119,097 shares, a Form 13F filed Thursday shows, a position dated to Sept. 30 that does not capture any trades the firm may have made since then.

Those caveats matter as much as the increase. Jane Street is a trading firm, and one of the largest market makers in stocks, ETFs, and options, and the same filing lists call options covering 13,573,000 Strategy shares and put options covering 13,991,800 shares, each more than three times the size of the common stake. Some of that stock is likely a hedge for the options and market-making book rather than a straight bet on the company. The 13F does not say how much.

The shares were valued at $630.6 million in the filing, using the Sept. 30 price of $153.09. Strategy has fallen about 25% since then. At Thursday’s close of $114.41, the same 4,119,097 shares were worth about $471 million.

The Class A position is up 1,441,475 shares from the 2,677,622 Jane Street reported at June 30, when the same holding was valued at $232.8 million. The firm filed about four days ahead of the Nov. 16 deadline; the SEC accepted the report at 4:04 p.m. Eastern on Thursday.

The options lines were already large in the second quarter and grew again. Calls covering 13,573,000 shares were valued at $2.08 billion in the new filing, and puts covering 13,991,800 shares, listed in two rows, were valued at $2.14 billion. Those figures use the same Sept. 30 share price as the common-stock line; a 13F reports the underlying shares the options cover, not the cash premium paid for the contracts.

The report is a holdings filing that folds in positions across seven other included managers, among them Jane Street Capital, Jane Street Options and Jane Street Global Trading, so the Strategy lines can include inventory across the group rather than a single proprietary bet. Authorized Signatory Jeremy Kahn signed the report in New York on Nov. 12. The table lists 14,764 entries valued at $1.19 trillion.

Jane Street’s 53.8% increase was the sharpest percentage increase among the large Strategy stakes in Thursday’s early 13F wave. Capital International Investors, a Capital Group unit, raised its stake 4.5% to 36,467,109 shares, worth about $4.17 billion at Thursday’s close. Invesco added 10.9%, to 10,362,698 shares, worth about $1.19 billion. Renaissance Technologies cut its position 41%, to 1,515,560 shares, worth about $173 million, and left the spot bitcoin ETF holdings it had reported at midyear, including BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust.

JPMorgan Chase, in a filing already reported, lifted its Strategy stake 36% to 848,932 shares and held 11.4 million IBIT shares, worth about $460 million at Thursday’s close. Bank of New York Mellon, in another early 13F, raised its stake in Strive (ASST). Those share counts, like Jane Street’s, are as of Sept. 30.

Still to file by the Nov. 16 deadline are the main quarterly 13Fs from Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, Millennium and Mubadala. Institutional managers that exercise investment discretion over $100 million or more in 13F securities have 45 days after quarter-end to disclose those holdings. That window ran to Nov. 14, a Saturday, so the deadline is Monday.

The Sept. 30 snapshot also precedes this week’s decision by MSCI to remove Strategy from its USA index, a step that will force index funds to sell by Nov. 30, and it precedes the stock’s slide since quarter-end. Monday’s remaining filings will show where other large holders stood on the same date, not where they stand now.

#5082. Andreessen Horowitz, DeFi Education Fund push back against Illinois crypto tax

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

A crypto cohort filed an amicus brief backing the crypto industry's challenge to Illinois's Digital Asset Tax Act on Thursday.

Crypto industry heavyweights are banding together to challenge Illinois over its controversial crypto tax, with the DeFi Education Fund and Andreessen Horowitz among those joining the fight.

On Thursday, the group filed an amicus brief backing the crypto industry's challenge to the Digital Asset Tax Act in Illinois, arguing that the law is overly broad and unfairly targets the digital asset industry.

"The Act singles out transactions involving blockchain technology for unfavorable tax treatment and affects a substantial number of Illinois residents: an estimated 2.64 million Illinoisans hold digital assets, the fifth-highest total of any state," they said in the brief.

The DeFi Education Fund, Paradigm, Uniswap and Andreessen Horowitz led the entities that signed the letter, which include  Aave Labs, Aptos Labs, Aztec Labs, Chainlink Labs, the Decentralization Research Center, Dromos Labs, Figment Inc., Hyperliquid Policy Center, Jito Labs, Multicoin Capital Management LLC, Orca Creative, Phantom Technologies, Solana Policy Institute and Stand With Crypto.

The filing adds to a growing legal battle over the legislation. The Blockchain Association, the Crypto Council for Innovation and The Digital Chamber have sued Illinois after its governor, JB Pritzker, signed into law the Digital Asset Tax Act as part of the state's fiscal year 2027 budget planning. The law imposes a 0.2% tax on digital asset transactions and was initially scheduled to go into effect in January 2027, but has since been postponed to July 2027.

The law has drawn swift criticism from crypto industry advocates, who have described it as unprecedented and sought to block its implementation. In the amicus brief on Thursday, the group said the law does not take into account the "technological complexities" that come with digital assets.

"The Act fails to establish sufficiently clear boundaries between taxable digital asset business activity and the provision of software and network infrastructure that enables users to transact independently," they said.

Cointelegraph reached out to Nvidia for comment but did not receive an immediate response.

#5079. India's Financial Crimes Agency Searches CoinDCX, Delta Exchange Over Offshore Trades

India's Financial Crimes Agency Searches CoinDCX, Delta Exchange Over Offshore Trades

India's Enforcement Directorate has searched three premises in Bengaluru linked to crypto exchanges CoinDCX and Delta Exchange, alleging the platforms carried out unauthorized cross-border transactions worth hundreds of millions of dollars through crypto derivatives and tokenized assets.

The searches were carried out on November 4 and 5 under India's Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), the agency, India's main financial crimes investigator, said in a statement dated November 6, which it shared on X on Thursday.

ईडी, बेंगलुरु ने क्रिप्टो टोकन डेरिवेटिव एवं वास्तविक विश्व परिसंपत्ति (आरडब्ल्यूए) टोकन से जुड़े बड़े पैमाने पर अनधिकृत सीमा-पार वित्तीय लेन-देन के मामले में फेमा, 1999 के अंतर्गत मेसर्स नेब्लियो टेक्नोलॉजीज प्राइवेट लिमिटेड (“कॉइनडीसीएक्स”) तथा मेसर्स एक्सेलियम टेक्नोलॉजीज… pic.twitter.com/vXwMU5uCTj

The investigation targets Neblio Technologies, which operates CoinDCX, and Excelium Technologies, which runs Delta Exchange. Both platforms offered futures and options on crypto tokens and openly advertised trading in tokenized shares of foreign companies, despite not being authorized to do so, according to the agency.

The Enforcement Directorate alleged that CoinDCX used stablecoins such as USDT to carry out trades for its users on foreign crypto exchanges, including Binance, KuCoin, Bybit and Gate.io, bypassing India's framework for sending money abroad.

When users deposited rupees and placed spot or derivatives orders, CoinDCX converted the funds into USDT and opened matching positions on the foreign exchanges, the agency said. The trades ran through accounts held in the name of a Costa Rica-based group entity, which it described as a shell entity, and were carried out directly from CoinDCX through APIs.

Trades in tokenized shares of companies such as Apple, Google and Tesla were in fact derivatives positions opened on decentralized exchange Hyperliquid, according to the agency.

The Enforcement Directorate alleged that the cross-border transactions in each of these areas ran to hundreds of millions of dollars. It also said CoinDCX holds crypto assets in enterprise accounts on foreign exchanges, and has paid tens of millions of dollars in fees to those platforms in USDT, in breach of FEMA.

The agency said a large share of Delta Exchange's derivatives trading was executed through CoinDCX, and that Delta Exchange had transferred tens of millions of dollars to its overseas group entities, which is under investigation. Their fintech products also appear to contravene India's securities and central banking laws, it said, adding that its investigation is ongoing.

CoinDCX said in a tweet on November 6 that Enforcement Directorate officials had visited its Bengaluru office "as part of a routine enquiry" on November 4, and that it had fully cooperated. The exchange said it operates "in strict compliance with all applicable Indian laws, including FEMA," and adheres to its KYC, anti-money laundering and reporting obligations as an entity registered with India's Financial Intelligence Unit. Trading, deposits and withdrawals on the platform remain fully operational, it added.

Officials from the Enforcement Directorate visited our Bengaluru office as part of a routine enquiry on 4th November. We fully cooperated with the authorities and provided all necessary information and assistance.

As an entity registered with FIU-IND, we regularly…

Delta Exchange said in its own tweet on November 6 that it had cooperated fully with officials, and that its business "does not involve any cross-border transfer of VDAs in any form or manner," using India's term for crypto assets. It said it runs a dedicated rupee-settled exchange for Indian users only, has been registered with India's Financial Intelligence Unit since 2023, and that trading, deposits and withdrawals were functioning normally.

The investigation adds to a difficult stretch for CoinDCX, one of India's largest crypto exchanges. Last month, it confirmed a $16 million loss from retired wallets, days after co-founder and CEO Sumit Gupta called reports of a breach a "false alarm." That followed a roughly $44 million exploit in July 2025.

Coinbase, which has invested in CoinDCX since 2020, won approval from India's competition regulator to increase its minority stake in the exchange's parent company.

#5077. Bitcoin Price Trades Near $71,200 After a Fresh Session Low as the 10-Year Yield Holds Above 5%

Bitcoin price traded near $71,200 on Thursday evening, after slipping to a fresh session low near $71,000, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro data. U.S. stocks closed modestly lower and the 10-year Treasury yield held above 5% after Nvidia said CEO Jensen Huang had resigned.

The decline started in the morning. Bitcoin price fell below $72,000 before the opening bell and dropped to about $71,150 shortly after 10 a.m. Eastern. A bounce later faded, and the bitcoin price printed a new session low near $71,000 around 6:23 p.m., its weakest level since Aug. 20. It now stands roughly $30,400 below where it closed one year ago.

The Nasdaq Composite slipped 0.39% to 25,366.09, the S&P 500 lost 0.35% to 7,592.40 and the Dow Jones Industrial Average fell 0.36% to 52,297.09, Reuters reported. Nvidia shares finished 4.3% lower at $166.19, off the day’s lows, after the company said Huang had resigned as president, chief executive and a board member, effective immediately, and named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Bitcoin-linked stocks had a harder session. Strategy (MSTR), which MSCI is removing from its USA Index at the Nov. 30 close, finished at $114.41, down 6.5%. Shares of BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT), which had bled $2 billion over nine straight days of outflows through Wednesday, closed at $40.36, down 4.5%.

The bond market did much of the afternoon work. The 10-year Treasury yield rose about 12 basis points to 5.105% and reached 5.12%, its highest since Oct. 21, after President Donald Trump said the United States does not need a deal with Iran, Reuters reported. The two-year yield, which tracks Federal Reserve expectations most closely, climbed about 14 basis points to 4.867%. Brent crude settled at $95.82 a barrel, up 3.3%, after attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure escalated.

Higher yields reflect a market that still expects the Fed to tighten again next month. Fed funds futures are pricing in 29 basis points of hikes in December, a quarter-point move as the base case with only slim odds of a half-point step, Reuters reported. Rising rates raise the cost of holding an asset that pays no interest, and that has weighed on the bitcoin price all week.

The Treasury added a fiscal reminder after the close. The government ran a $404 billion deficit in October, the first month of fiscal 2027, the Treasury Department said, up 42% from $284 billion a year earlier, as receipts fell to $369 billion and outlays rose to $773 billion. Some benefit payments that would normally have been made on Nov. 1, a Sunday, were shifted into October.

The evening also brought a cluster of crypto headlines. The Justice Department said it has court authority to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of cryptocurrency stolen from Binance in October’s North Korea-linked heist, part of a freeze that officials said halted about half the breach. Binance, in a separate announcement, said it has fully covered the $6.34 billion in user losses with company funds. Jane Street’s quarterly 13F showed it had raised its Strategy stake 54% as of Sept. 30, though large options positions point to hedging rather than a straight bet. After the close, Gemini reported third-quarter revenue of $40.9 million, down 19%, as platform trading volume fell 73%; its net loss narrowed 63% to $58.6 million.

Those announcements did not lift the bitcoin price off the session lows. Friday’s Producer Price Index is the next inflation test, and Nvidia is still scheduled to report quarterly results on Nov. 17 under its interim leadership.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $71,200.
