#5042. Tether Gold Gets Clean KPMG Audit Confirming $2.2B in Physical Gold Backing

Tether Gold Gets Clean KPMG Audit Confirming $2.2B in Physical Gold Backing

Tether's gold-backed stablecoin, Tether Gold (XAUT), has completed its first full audit by KPMG, which found the token fully backed by physical gold, the company announced on Thursday.

KPMG issued an unqualified opinion on the financial statements of TG Commodities, which issues XAUT, for the year ended December 31, 2025. An unqualified, or "clean," opinion means the auditor found that the statements fairly present the company's financial position, without reservations.

Tether Gold (XAU₮) Becomes Tether’s Second Stablecoin to Complete a Big Four AuditLearn more: https://t.co/eO0asUwvlI

As of the end of 2025, TG Commodities held 520,089.350 troy ounces of allocated gold, valued at $2.246 billion, against 520,089.300 XAUT tokens in issue, according to Tether. The gold is held in vaults in Switzerland, and each token represents an ownership interest in gold on specific bars.

As part of the audit, KPMG physically counted and inspected every gold bar backing the token, rather than relying only on confirmations from custodians, Tether said.

"KPMG counted every single bar as part of its Tether Gold audit," Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said, adding that he believes XAUT "is on track to become the first true competitor to the gold ETF market."

The XAUT audit is Tether's second by a Big Four firm. In August, KPMG issued a clean opinion on Tether International, the issuer of its flagship USDT stablecoin, in what the company called the largest inaugural audit in history, Decrypt reported at the time.

For years, Tether faced criticism for relying on quarterly attestations, which offer a snapshot of reserves at a single point in time, rather than a full audit of its finances. The company first said earlier this year that it was being audited by a Big Four firm without naming it, before confirming KPMG.

Tether still publishes quarterly attestations for USDT. Its latest, prepared by accounting firm BDO and covering the period to September 30, showed total reserve assets of $191.5 billion against $183.5 billion in liabilities, giving it an excess reserve buffer of almost $8 billion, according to the report. Tether said it made $1.5 billion in net operating profit in the third quarter.

"These milestones advance the progress towards audits across the whole of Tether," said chief financial officer Simon McWilliams.

Separately from the gold backing XAUT, Tether has also built up its own large gold reserves, and Ardoino has said it will keep adding to its stash, which it stores in a former nuclear bunker in Switzerland.

#5039. Avalanche Owns Five Percent of Tokenized Stocks

Sign up to get 2 days early access to each edition of Tokenization Briefing

Over this past week, three U.S. tokenized stock venues picked three different blockchains to launch on. The WSJ reported Monday that NYSE chose Avalanche as the sole settlement chain for its planned 24/7 platform. On November 4, $CNTN became the first U.S. equity to trade under the SEC's Innovation Exemption. Five North sold tokenized $CNTN shares to DRW Cumberland on TSV, a permissioned venue on the Canton Network. On Monday, Formation notified the SEC that it will run a venue on Solana, targeting early 2027.

Given the strict regulations surrounding tokenized stocks in the U.S., no venue is going multichain at launch. Besides increasing complexity for regulatory compliance, fracturing activity across different networks would make it difficult to build deep liquidity while staying under the SEC's strict 0.25% average daily volume cap per asset.

The leading chains by tokenized stock volume have benefited from large exchanges launching their own tokenized stock products and leveraging their existing user bases.

The chains that successfully gain market share will be the ones that attract the biggest platforms and issuers.

Tokenized stocks have matched the growth of stablecoins closely. 240 days after crossing $1 billion, stablecoins stood at roughly $4 billion and tokenized stocks stood at $3.7 billion, while tokenized Treasuries were at $2.5 billion at the same point and have since fallen well behind. Although tokenized stocks may not continue at this growth rate, the trend is promising.

The count of tokenized stocks and ETFs worth more than $1 million climbed from three in Jan 2025 to 287 as of Nov 8, across roughly 4,400 tokenized assets and a $3.7 billion market.

Distribution keeps broadening too. HTX launched spot trading in bStocks on Monday with 20 names including NVDA, TSLA, QQQ, and CRCL. Strategy and SpaceX are now the most tokenized issuers by asset count. Our take is that breadth is the healthier signal for a young market, because a long tail of products with real size offers a better user experience than one or two large wrappers.

#5037. Anchorage Digital’s Stablecoins Can Now Settle Payments on Visa’s Network

Anchorage Digital’s Stablecoins Can Now Settle Payments on Visa’s Network

Anchorage Digital is extending its stablecoin reach into one of the world’s largest payment networks, teaming up with Visa to let select Anchorage-issued tokens settle transactions directly on VisaNet, starting with Western Union’s USDPT.

Under the arrangement announced Thursday, Visa and eligible companies can now use USDPT, a dollar-pegged stablecoin issued and custodied by Anchorage Digital Bank, to settle their obligations on the Visa network.

It’s the first Anchorage-issued token to gain that status, and the companies said the deal establishes a framework to add more of its stablecoins later.

The appeal, Anchorage argues, is pairing the speed of a stablecoin with the backing of a regulated bank. Anchorage operates what it bills as America’s first federally chartered crypto bank, overseen by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

“Speed alone won’t bring institutions onchain,” said Nathan McCauley, Anchorage’s co-founder and CEO, arguing that institutions want “a regulated institution behind the money,” now getting both a stablecoin’s speed and a federally regulated bank’s safeguards.

The move is the latest sign of Visa weaving stablecoins into its settlement plumbing.

The payments giant has built out a stablecoin settlement program spanning networks like Base, Polygon and others, launched a platform to help banks and fintech firms issue and move stablecoins, and has moved into onchain lending tied to its cards.

Dan Roesbery, a Visa vice president for crypto partnerships, framed the USDPT support as giving partners “another trusted, institution-grade option” for settling with Visa.

For Anchorage, the deal advances a broader ambition to become the settlement bank for stablecoins at institutional scale, not just issuing them but providing the custody and settlement rails underneath.

The bank already issues a roster of institution-grade stablecoins, including Tether’s U.S.-regulated USAT, Western Union’s USDPT, Ethena’s USDtb and others, each redeemable one-to-one for dollars. Its counterparty settlement network, Atlas, handles off-exchange settlement, collateral management and cross-border transfers.

Anchorage also recently positioned itself as the custody backbone of the U.S.-Mexico stablecoin corridor, custodying tokens on both sides of one of the world’s largest remittance routes. Backing a token on Visa’s network, which reaches merchants and banks worldwide, extends that institutional footprint further into mainstream payments.

The companies said support for additional Anchorage-issued stablecoins could follow the initial USDPT launch.

#5033. Louisiana sues Kalshi, Robinhood and other prediction markets in escalating regulatory fight

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill said she filed a lawsuit against platforms Kalshi, Robinhood, Polymarket, Forecastex, Crypto.com, Coinbase, Novig, Underdog, Gemini and Prophetx.

Louisiana is the latest state to take on prediction market platforms, joining a growing legal battle between states and the federal government over who has jurisdiction to regulate the industry.

On Thursday, Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill said she filed a lawsuit against platforms Kalshi, Robinhood, Polymarket, Forecastex, Crypto.com, Coinbase, Novig, Underdog, Gemini and Prophetx for breaking the law.

"For too long, they have violated our laws with impunity," Murrill said in a post on X. "No longer: I am seeking an immediate restraining order and an injunction to restore the rule of law in Louisiana."

Louisiana is now the 11th state to sue one or more prediction markets following Arkansas, Utah and Connecticut, among others. And platforms have fired back. Following the lawsuit on Thursday, Kalshi filed an emergency temporary restraining order in preparation.

"Based on recent discussions with representatives of the state of Louisiana, Kalshi believes that the State will initiate an action against Kalshi in state court to restrict Kalshi from operating in Louisiana," Kalshi said in its court filing.

Prediction markets became massively popular following the 2024 presidential elections, with some of the biggest platforms now worth billions of dollars. States have generally taken issue specifically with sports betting and say that platforms are operating illegally and are avoiding paying taxes. The issue also brings up concerns among tribal organizations about how the platforms could circumvent agreements.

Meanwhile, platforms have argued that the Commodity Futures Trading Commission should be in charge of regulating the platforms, including sports betting. The CFTC has also sued states to get "exclusive jurisdiction," which could later make its way up to the Supreme Court.

On Thursday, a Robinhood spokesperson asserted that its event contracts are federally regulated by the CFTC in response to Louisiana's lawsuit.

"As we've previously shared, Robinhood's event contracts are federally regulated by the CFTC and offered through Robinhood Derivatives, LLC, a CFTC-registered entity, allowing retail customers to access prediction markets in a safe, compliant, and regulated manner," the spokesperson told The Block.

A Coinbase spokesperson called states' approach fragmented.

"Louisiana's lawsuit, filed while multiple federal appellate courts actively consider the same issues, is another example of the fragmented enforcement Congress sought to prevent when it gave the CFTC exclusive jurisdiction over event contracts," the spokesperson said in an emailed statement.

The Coalition for Prediction Markets, which represents several of the platforms sued on Thursday, said the lawsuit was "troubling and unlawful."

"Louisiana’s attack on this industry is a troubling and unlawful attempt to assert state authority over markets governed by federal law that would only cut off people’s access to an important tool for gathering market information, expressing informed views, and hedging risks based on their own expertise," that spokesperson said. "That cannot stand."

Separately, CNBC reported on Wednesday that the CFTC is investigating whether Kalshi failed to inform the CFTC properly about its proposed event contracts.  This comes soon after Reuters reported that the CFTC is investigating Polymarket over whether it violated a deal made in 2022 to promise to ban U.S. users from its flagship overseas platform.

Updated at 10:10 p.m. UTC to include comments

#5032. Are Alt-L1s over? Security, costs drive smaller chains to Ethereum

L1 blockchains Zilliqa, Gnosis and Ronin join the Ethereum ecosystem as the costs of going it alone grow, and the security and liquidity advantages become too hard to resist.

Running an independent blockchain has always come with a particular promise: control.

A standalone Layer 1 architecture allows projects to be the masters of their own destiny, set the rules of consensus, maintain their own validators and build infrastructure around their users, without relying on any other network.

But with great power comes great responsibility — and considerable expense.

Blockchains need enough users, developers and economic activity to support the infrastructure that secures them. For some smaller networks, keeping an entire ecosystem afloat no longer makes economic sense.

Zilliqa is the latest project to confront that reality, announcing plans last week to move away from its L1 architecture and become an Ethereum-based Layer 2 (L2) instead:

“Usage never reached what a standalone chain needs,” the post explained. “Funding and liquidity for standalone L1s dried up. The recent hacks showed how much it takes to secure a chain alone. The data is onchain for everyone to see.”

Blockchain ecosystem Gnosis (which has always been Ethereum-adjacent) is also working to transition Gnosis Chain into an Ethereum-based network through its Ethereum Economic Zone (EEZ) initiative. Its RollupX test network intends to show how participating chains can share Ethereum settlement and synchronous composability as native rollups.

Gaming-focused Ronin blockchain also completed its move back to Ethereum as an L2 in May.

With interest in alt L1s drying up along with the funding, will we start to see more and more chains consolidating in the Ethereum ecosystem?

Independent L1s generally have to keep several core functions going, including execution, consensus, data availability and settlement. They must also fund the token incentives used to secure their networks through staking.

Related: What are native rollups? Full guide to Ethereum’s latest innovation

“It comes down to a chain’s profitability,” Max Shannon, senior research analyst at Bitwise, tells Magazine.

ZIL becomes a Layer 2 on Ethereum. Source: Zilliqa

“L2s only need to maintain execution and do not necessarily need a token, which makes them a more attractive financial and operational proposition,” he says.

Shannon says the top ten largest projects are more financially sustainable. Coinbase and Robinhood’s L2 networks generated $48 million and $52 million in fees respectively over the last 365 days, with margins above 90%. The next eight largest chains each generated more than $1 million annually.

From 11th place onward, however, annual fees collectively amounted to less than $1 million. So a network may have ambitious technology and a dedicated community, but those things don’t necessarily pay the bills.

Ethereum is often criticized for giving away too much of its revenue to L2s, but that’s precisely what makes it attractive to set up projects there.

But just becoming an Ethereum L2 doesn’t automatically make a network profitable however. Shannon points to Optimism, Katana and Linea as examples of networks whose expenses outweigh their fee revenue. Projects like Blast have already been forced to shut down.

Gnosis has targeted spring 2027 to complete its transition, which will see it give up its independent validator-based consensus in favor of Ethereum settlement.

Gnosis Chain will relaunch as an Ethereum Economic Zone. Source: Gnosis

“Two years ago I gave a talk about ‘native Rollups’ that would truly extend Ethereum instead of being isolated chains,” co-founder Martin Köppelmann said, adding that RollupX “even goes beyond what I had imagined back then!”

Gnosis’s EEZ initiative aims to enable separate Ethereum-based networks interact synchronously, so a smart contract on one network can call another and use its response within the same transaction, without relying on the usual bridge-and-wait process.

That’s a prospect that has some Ethereum supporters particularly bullish, with ETH bull materkel describing the EEZ as a potential “black hole for liquidity,” and saying it could allow participating networks to tap into Ethereum’s total value locked (TVL) and strengthen its position as a liquidity hub.

Could network effects, and increased interoperability draw more and more chains into Ethereum’s orbit?

It isn’t only the operating cost economics that make sense. Projects can also tap into an established ecosystem of applications and developers rather than building those network effects from scratch.

Alex Gluchowski, co-founder and chief executive of Matter Labs, the company behind Ethereum L2 ZKsync, says he expects more L1 teams to maintain their own chains while using Ethereum for settlement.

“Running an independent L1 means maintaining its security as well as building something people want to use,” Gluchowski tells Magazine.

But Gluchowski says sovereignty and Ethereum integration are not mutually exclusive, since chains can retain control over their rules and operations while using Ethereum for shared settlement.

Ronin completed migration to Ethereum in May. Source: Ronin

And for applications handling sensitive financial information, maintaining a private environment under their own control can be essential, he says. Shared settlement does not have to mean exposing confidential transaction data.

The biggest obstacle today is that all the L2s are effectively isolated from each other and from Ethereum. It’s certainly possible to jump between them, but its far from seamless and interoperability is poor.

Shannon cautions against describing the market as undergoing straightforward consolidation around Ethereum.

Some large institutions are launching L2 networks, he says, including Coinbase and Robinhood, but others are still choosing to build independent L1s, like Circle’s Arc, Stripe’s Tempo, or Hyperliquid’s purpose-specific perpetual futures blockchain.

“So there is no clear conclusion to the L1 versus L2 debate as yet,” he says.

Magazine: ‘Bunker mode’ won’t protect your crypto, critics say

#5031. The Guy’s Guide to Adult Acne

We’re all stressed AF. The food we’re eating probably isn’t helping. Everyone told us we wouldn’t have to worry about our hormones going crazy after our teen years. What the heck is going on?

THE YEAR WAS 1997. I’d just made the varsity baseball team as a freshman, and I was ready to shoot my shot with every senior girl who’d give me the time of day. The only problem: I had also hard-launched my pepperoni face. I’m not talking surface-level New York slice stuff—these breakouts were Detroit-style-pizza deep. Every clogged pore had clocked in at the same time, and I was an absolute wreck.

For six months, I’d burned through everything the CVS aisles had to offer: Oxy Pads, Stridex, Noxzema, whatever stuff was hawking a promise of clear skin. Nothing worked. My face just got redder, drier, and more irritated. My mom (thank god for her) dragged me to the dermatologist, and I walked out with a prescription for isotretinoin, a.k.a. Accutane. Five months later, my skin was completely clear. My mini CVS stash went into the trash, and I thought that’d be the end of it.

I was wrong. I spent years assuming that because I took Accutane as a teenager, I’d paid my dues, but as a 44-year-old dude, I still get the occasional pimple or two—seemingly right before a big work meeting or whenever I have a lot of stuff on my plate. And it still messes with my head. Does my hygiene suck? Are my hormones to blame? Am I sweating too much? Apparently, I’m not the only one.

According to Christopher Bunick, MD, PhD, an associate professor of dermatology at the Yale School of Medicine, 83 percent of teen boys will deal with acne before they reach their 20s, which is often due to a surge in testosterone at the time. And while acne typically clears up as testosterone levels stabilize in our 20s, it can make a surprise comeback in our 30s, 40s, 50s, and beyond—with around 20 percent of men in their 30s dealing with breakouts, but only 12 percent seeking treatment from a medical professional. It’s not just a skin issue: Research shows that adult acne is consistently linked to a higher risk of developing anxiety, depression, and reduced self-esteem.

Acne (not to be mistaken for folliculitis, which can result from shaving) is mainly caused by a combination of excess oil production and dead skin cells that clog pores. This can create an environment where strains of Cutibacterium acnes—the bacteria naturally found on your skin—become imbalanced and trigger inflammation. This could look like blackheads and whiteheads, raised pustules and papules, or the deeper, more painful nodules or cysts that are more likely to scar. And despite what your instincts tell you, excessively scrubbing your face won’t help—it could actually harm your skin barrier. “You cannot wash away hormones,” says Dr. Bunick. Acne is an inflammatory condition, not a hygiene problem.

Scientists still aren’t sure exactly what makes some guys more susceptible to acne than others, but genetics and hormones are two leading factors. Research suggests about 80 percent of your acne risk is inherited. Your genes determine how sensitive your skin is to the hormones (i.e., androgens) that regulate the sebaceous glands and how aggressively your immune system overreacts to fight the inflammation. Then there are the lifestyle factors that play a smaller role but can collectively add up, such as diet (Dr. Bunick is a believer in the gut-skin axis—the idea that an imbalanced gut microbiome can drive systemic inflammation that can predispose you to acne), stress (this spikes cortisol, which also drives oil production and contributes to inflammation), sleep deprivation (same), and testosterone replacement therapy.

Though there isn’t a cure for acne yet, there are plenty of treatment options. “Untreated acne doesn’t just affect your skin. It affects how you move through the world,” says Dr. Bunick. “Acne impacts confidence, whether it’s making friends, having a social or dating life, or performance in work, sports, or other extracurriculars.” Earlier intervention means less of a psychosocial toll, less scarring, and (you heard the man) more confidence.

So if your bathroom still looks like mine did in the ’90s, it’s time to investigate what might be causing the chaos in your largest organ and learn how to treat it. The stories below will help you get your skin clear and, hopefully, keep it that way.

Bob Gearing is a New York-based writer covering men’s grooming from the perspective of a very real (and very sweaty) dude. By day, he’s a well-SPF’d tech sales bro; by night, he’s a red-light mask guy who definitely didn’t think he’d care this much about skincare. When he’s not testing the latest face serum, you’ll find him sweating through a round of golf or holding out hope that the Lions will make the Super Bowl before he dies.

#5030. Tron, Tether and Chainalysis Form a New Crypto-Crime Task Force—Replacing One That Just Collapsed

Tron, Tether and Chainalysis Form a New Crypto-Crime Task Force—Replacing One That Just Collapsed

Tron, Tether, and blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis have launched a joint effort to hunt down and disrupt criminal activity involving the USDT stablecoin, just weeks after a nearly identical alliance, with a different analytics partner, quietly fell apart.

Announced Wednesday, the Digital Crime Task Force aims to identify illicit activity involving Tether’s USDT on the Tron blockchain and surface it for law enforcement before criminals can move their funds.

The pitch is a shift from reactive to proactive: rather than waiting for a victim to report a crime or an investigator to flag tainted funds, the group says it will proactively trace criminal networks and coordinate asset freezes, drawing on Chainalysis’s intelligence and AI-powered investigative tools. It will also accept tips and freeze requests from police.

“Criminals are using AI to move faster than ever, and waiting for a victim to report a crime is no longer good enough,” said Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino, noting the company has frozen more than $7 billion tied to illicit activity while working with over 360 law enforcement agencies across 73 countries.

Tron founder Justin Sun framed it as protecting the millions who rely on USDT, which circulates on Tron to the tune of more than $94 billion.

The notable wrinkle is what this new alliance replaces. Tron and Tether previously ran a similar operation, the T3 Financial Crime Unit, formed in 2024 with rival analytics firm TRM Labs and later joined by Binance. Together, the group froze more than $450 million in tainted assets.

But Bloomberg reported earlier this month that T3 was quietly disbanded in late August, with people familiar citing slow response times and a lack of coordination among its members. Its collapse went public just days before the record $6.4 billion Binance hack.

Swapping TRM for Chainalysis is a pointed move, given the two are fierce competitors. Chainalysis has even sued the U.S. government over an ICE contract awarded to TRM without competitive bidding, underscoring the rivalry now running through crypto’s crime-fighting infrastructure.

The new task force says it will target a broad sweep of threats, from romance and investment scams to stolen funds, terrorist financing, North Korean cyber operations, cartel money laundering and child-exploitation material.

The group also framed the effort around a coming era of autonomous AI agents transacting onchain, which it warned will open fresh avenues for criminal exploitation.

Shares of Gemini Space Station Inc. Gemini Space Station Inc. (GEMI) rose 6.5% in after-hours trading to $2.79 but remain down around 75% year to date.

#5026. The Paths to Clear(er) Skin

With a little trust and patience, it’s possible to find a solution that works for you. These three guys are proof.

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

IT’S ONE THING to get advice from a doctor (still important and invaluable), but it’s another to hear from other guys who’ve been there. Guys who understand what it’s like to cancel plans because of a whopper of a zit. Guys who’ve also hesitated to take their shirts off at the pool for fear of being judged for their bacne. We wanted to hear about their struggles, and successes—what finally worked to help them send breakouts packing and regain their confidence. As you’ll learn from these three men, no two plans are the same. But with a little consistency and some experimentation, blemish-free skin is possible.

I like to take care of myself. I work out at least five times a week. I’m consistent with my diet. But my confidence takes a hit because of my acne. Since I was a teenager, I’ve dealt with deep cystic breakouts that have totally upended my life. I’ve canceled dates; I’ve canceled trips; I won’t leave the house when I have a flare. It’s improved as I’ve gotten older—since 2023 I’ve been using Winlevi, a prescription hormone-blocking cream, in the morning and at night, and Tazorac, a doctor-dispensed retinoid that I’ve been using since my 20s, at night—but I still deal with the occasional breakout. And it still messes with my confidence.

I went to Greece over the summer, and I could feel a big one coming under my skin. The one thing that really makes a difference is the La Roche-Posay Effaclar Duo+M treatment, which you can get for $25 at the drugstore. I apply it religiously every few hours as soon as I feel a zit coming on, and sometimes it helps it come to a head faster or stops it from forming altogether. I’ve found a routine that works, but acne is always something lingering in the back of my mind. —Derek Monacchio, 38, tax director

Back acne is something I’ve had my entire life, but honestly, I just thought it was something I’d always have to deal with. I couldn’t reach back there with a cream or a wash, and I didn’t want to go on Accutane or any prescription medications. Even though most people couldn’t see it, I’d get very self-conscious whenever I’d take my shirt off in public, which sucked because I take good care of my body.

That all changed when I went in for a laser hair removal appointment at a physician-led laser center, The Kaga Institute, about six months ago. The office staff knew my situation with the breakouts on my back and told me they had something that could help—a new laser called AviClear, which supposedly suppresses oil gland production. I was curious, so I gave it a try. After three sessions, the pimples on my back were gone. It was totally painless for me—especially when compared to the laser hair removal I’ve tried. Each click of the laser felt like a warm sensation for a millisecond, and I was able to continue with my laser hair removal at the same time. Now that I’ve finished my series of treatments, I was told the results should improve with time. It feels great to have found a solution that works. I’m ready for next summer already. —William Sebok, 42, construction company owner

There’s a perception that when you have acne, you find a solution and then it’s over. That has not been my experience. My first breakouts (a mix of whiteheads and inflamed red bumps) started just as I was hitting puberty, around age 11, and persisted well into my 30s. I’ve seen a few different dermatologists since I was 15 and was prescribed everything under the sun—from clindamycin (a prescription antibiotic) to Accutane. I would take the medications for a few months at a time, and some would help for a while, but once I stopped, my skin would inevitably break out again. Plus, I experienced some side effects from the Accutane, including significant lethargy.

After I stopped taking the medications, I started looking at the labels of the skin care products I was using and noticed that when the ingredient list was extremely long, there was a higher likelihood there would be something in there that would clog my pores. [Editor’s note: Look for the label “noncomedogenic,” a.k.a. not pore clogging, on your products.] For me, it became more about avoiding certain products and soothing the skin barrier. I also became much more careful about not overloading my routine with too many strong ingredients. I focused instead on making sure the products I was using were appropriate for my acne-prone skin. That was the game changer. Another thing that causes my skin to react is my diet. If I’m eating a lot of high-glycemic foods and meals that are high in sugar, I immediately break out. I also try to wash my face or shower immediately after every workout, and if I can’t, I spray on a hypochlorous acid spray to kill the bacteria. Less is more, and I’m proof of that. —Brute Choi, 37, content creator

Brian Underwood is beauty director at Women’s Health, where he oversees content strategy for the brand across all platforms, including digital, print, and social. Underwood previously served as beauty and wellness director at Oprah Daily and O, The Oprah Magazine. During his tenure leading beauty content for the Oprah brand at Hearst, stories Underwood commissioned were awarded the Skin Cancer Foundation Media Award and a Fragrance Award for Editorial Excellence (his second). #5024. 'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang is out as CEO after 33 years, and some of the loudest names in crypto and artificial intelligence think the company just made a spectacular mistake.

On Thursday, Nvidia confirmed Huang’s resignation as president, CEO, and board member, and named longtime finance chief Colette Kress interim CEO. The move followed reports of two extramarital affairs, including one involving a businesswoman U.S. intelligence reportedly suspected of links to China, as well as concerns over a reported relationship with an Nvidia employee.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees on Sunday. Nvidia said in a board statement that it had no reason to believe its secrets or national security were compromised, and its independent investigation remains underway. The allegations about foreign intelligence have not established that Huang knowingly provided information to a foreign government.

But even without any leaks, the scandal was enough for the board to decide to let him go.

The market did not welcome the transition. Nvidia shares fell 14% at Thursday’s opening bell; with more than $500 billion wiped from U.S. stocks in a single minute. The company that was once the most valuable in the world has now fallen to third on that list.

Nvidia was already under pressure before the scandal, and Huang’s departure raised a particular problem for a company built so closely around its founder: Who, exactly, can replace him?

Changpeng “CZ” Zhao, the Binance co-founder and one of crypto’s most influential executives, said “it's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.” Bitcoin entrepreneur and former Kraken executive Dan Held agreed, writing, “He shouldn't have resigned,” and argued that Huang’s personal problems should be weighed against the company he built.

It's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 12, 2026

U.S. intelligence officials reportedly assessed Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset, but Nvidia says it has no reason to believe any secrets were compromised, and Singaporean authorities said they lacked sufficient evidence to establish that she committed a crime. As of today, no law enforcement agency has presented any hard evidence against Alice Huang.

Anthony Pompliano, the Bitcoin investor and commentator, moved the conversation from the boardroom to the technology itself. “Do not get distracted by the headlines,” he wrote. “Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.”

Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.

— Anthony Pompliano 🌪 (@APompliano) November 12, 2026

Surrounding it all is likely the most talked about conspiracy theory in the open-source AI sphere. The ousting was designed to hurt open-source developments, the speculation goes, since Nvidia is one of the leading voices in the open-source movement, which closed-source companies see as a threat.

For Voorhees, the controversy also prompted a practical question. “Is there a decentralized Hugging Face yet?” he asked, alluding to one of Huang’s stated commitments. In other words, the crypto solution to a potential platform bottleneck is to stop trusting a single platform.

That concern is more than a slogan for a founder whose Venice AI business is built around private access to open models. Voorhees previously told Decrypt that the antidote to excessive control over AI was “open-source decentralization.”

Some developers saw a deeper threat in Huang’s departure: the loss of a powerful corporate champion of downloadable AI models, whose underlying parameters, or weights, can be run outside the companies that built them.

The worry is that an Nvidia without Huang could become less willing to confront firms that keep their most advanced models behind proprietary services.

Jun Song, an open-source AI advocate and hardware entrepreneur, alleged that OpenAI and Anthropic had spent millions lobbying Congress to restrict open weights. He pointed to a “Pace the Frontier” campaign and argued that Huang responded by building the Open Secure AI Alliance, ending his post with, “And today, we lost him.”

OpenAI and Anthropic have spent millions lobbying the US Congress to ban open weights.

When they formed the "Pace the Frontier" group to push for strict limits on open models, Jensen stepped up right away.

He created the Open Secure Alliance to fight back.

Look at the list of… pic.twitter.com/OeasotcLO3

In July, Nvidia joined Microsoft, IBM, Hugging Face, and dozens of other organizations to launch the Open Secure AI Alliance, which works on open AI security tools and standards. Nvidia also backed a letter defending open weights against broad restrictions.

The broader conspiracy claim needs, of course, much more evidence. The Pacing the Frontier initiative is a statement from employees at several AI companies seeking tools to slow dangerous frontier development, not proof of a joint corporate campaign to ban all open-weight models or remove Huang.

OpenAI subsequently signed the pro-open-weight letter, while Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said explicitly that his company had never advocated such a ban.

Other posts on social media showed how much anxiety the debate had generated. A commenter replied to Song that they were now “counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community.”

Huawei and Deepseek are two major Chinese companies, known for their open-source developments

I can't believe now I am counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community. What a loss...

— Look4Newways (@sflk2026) November 12, 2026

The issue comes with a rather large business backdrop. Nvidia agreed in September to acquire the open-source AI repository Hugging Face for roughly $12.9 billion, subject to regulatory clearance, in a deal expected to close in the first half of 2027.

That arrangement could put both the hardware used to build AI and the largest hub used to distribute open models under one company’s influence—arguably, the most powerful company on earth.

Epic Games founder and CEO Tim Sweeney was among the highest-profile tech executives to condemn the exit. “A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity,” he wrote, praising Huang for keeping innovation moving even as Nvidia grew into an enormous company.

A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity. Jensen founded and ran one of the few companies to reach a massive scale while driving massive continued innovation and ubiquity, while others fell into the trap of ever-increasing extraction. https://t.co/AkS5XT9HF8

— Tim Sweeney (@TimSweeneyEpic) November 12, 2026

Perplexity AI co-founder and CEO Aravind Srinivas took a more personal approach. “Thank you Jensen Huang for 33 years of bold leadership,” he wrote. He also credited Huang’s generosity toward other founders, including himself, for helping shape the future of American AI.

Peter Steinberger, creator of the open-source OpenClaw agent and now at OpenAI, argued that the decision felt rushed. “If that spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal,” he wrote on X. “Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.”

If that Spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal. Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.

— Peter Steinberger (@steipete) November 12, 2026

Financial commentator The Kobeissi Letter had urged the board to consult shareholders before any decision, arguing that there was no obvious replacement and citing research on the costs of ousting successful CEOs. After the announcement, the account called the resignation “the most costly mistake of the AI Revolution.”

Former DWS executive and corporate-governance critic Desiree Fixler invoked directors’ fiduciary duty, saying the board should protect shareholders instead of indulging what she called a media frenzy.

In general, most of the reactions were supportive, separating personal affairs from business. The corporate espionage angle has apparently not swayed tech enthusiasts.

This Jensen Huang thing is so ugly. Guy takes a company from zero to absolute pinnacle, through blood, sweat, and tears, a pure founder’s founder, great products, makes so many people rich beyond their imaginations, champions open free local AI, and then the incumbent modern…

— Neil Woodfine (@nwoodfine) November 12, 2026

Not everyone outside that chorus thought the security questions could be set aside. North Dakota Republican Sen. Kevin Cramer had called for Huang’s exit, warning about the risk of blackmail and saying the internal investigation was insufficient. The concern from Washington has been largely uniform: the possibility of compromising pressure against the leader of a company central to U.S. AI infrastructure.

Meanwhile, President Donald Trump has remained supportive of Huang: “He’s definitely suffering,” he told the press. “I think he’ll be in good shape.”

Speculation about Huang’s next job spread just as quickly as criticism of his last one. Responding to an X user asking him to hire Huang, Elon Musk replied, “That would be an honor.”

Nvidia says its board is searching for a permanent CEO from inside and outside the company, with Kress running the business in the meantime. Its next scheduled earnings report is November 17, the first major financial update investors will receive after Huang’s departure.

#5023. DOJ moves to seize $1.2 billion from Binance hack, disrupts North Korean deepfake scheme

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The DOJ received court approval to seize more than $1.2 billion stolen from Binance and shut down systems used in a separate North Korean scheme involving fake video calls.

The U.S. Department of Justice has obtained court approval to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion stolen from crypto exchange Binance and disrupted a separate North Korean scheme that used fake video calls to steal crypto.

The Binance funds are part of more than $2.7 billion frozen through efforts by U.S. authorities and private companies, the DOJ said Thursday. Investigators are still tracing stolen assets as the hackers move them through crypto bridges, mixers and exchanges, the department said.

The DOJ attributed the Binance hack to North Korean military hackers known as TraderTraitor. The group stole more than $6 billion from the exchange around Oct. 29, according to the department.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," said Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor. "The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

Binance previously put its losses at about $6.3 billion and set aside $6.34 billion in company funds to cover them. Earlier Thursday, Binance said it has completed coverage of assets impacted in the hack and published a new proof of reserves snapshot.

Separately, the DOJ said it seized cloud infrastructure used in a North Korean campaign the FBI calls FaceStealer. The scheme has led to more than $400 million in crypto theft over the past 18 months, the department said.

Hackers used stolen accounts to invite victims to video calls, where they played recordings or deepfakes of familiar people. They then faked technical problems and tricked victims into installing malware that could steal crypto or access other systems, the DOJ said.

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships," said Patrick Grandy, assistant director in charge of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office. "If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough."

In some cases, the theft and takeover of communication accounts happened months to more than a year after the computer was first compromised, the department said.

Hackers also used stolen contacts and accounts to target more people, including open-source software developers, potentially to carry out attacks through their software. The FBI seized more than 900 webcam videos of FaceStealer victims and issued guidance for those affected, the DOJ said.

#5021. Can Acne Be Prevented With a Vaccine?

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

WHAT IF ACNE wasn’t so damn frustrating to treat or just…didn’t exist at all? George Liu, MD, PhD, and a team of researchers at the University of California, San Diego, are developing a vaccine designed to target certain types of bacteria to help prevent acne from forming in the first place.

Dr. Liu’s team researches how bacterial pathogens cause diseases. While doing so, they discovered that C. acnes—a bacteria that naturally lives on the skin and whose imbalance is a known acne trigger—secretes two forms of an enzyme called hyaluronidase: Hyl-A and Hyl-B. Hyl-A plays a central role in the development of acne by causing inflammation, whereas Hyl-B is an anti-inflammatory that can be beneficial for skin by reducing inflammation and helping prevent an aggressive immune response.

Existing acne treatments are broad and often focus on killing all C. acnes strains, which can take several months and introduce significant side effects that can range anywhere from dryness, redness, peeling, and irritation to antibiotic resistance. Instead, Dr. Liu’s vaccine—which has been tested only on rodents so far—aims to teach the immune system to block just Hyl-A, leaving the benefits of Hyl-B and preventing inflammatory acne from forming in the first place.

UC San Diego isn’t the only group chasing an acne vaccine. Paris-based pharmaceutical company Sanofi is developing an mRNA vaccine to treat active breakouts rather than prevent future ones. “The idea is to target two of the key contributors to this inflammatory cycle—the [C. acnes] bacterial strains that cause inflammation and the associated toxins—and aim to halt the disease process,” says Jean-François Toussaint, PhD, global head of vaccine research and development at Sanofi. The vaccine is currently being tested in adults with moderate to severe acne during a phase I/II clinical trial.

These vaccines could potentially do wonders for chronic acne sufferers and help save hundreds of dollars per year on acne products and medications. Between his team’s research and Sanofi’s developments, Dr. Liu predicts an acne vaccine could be available to the public within the next five years.

#5020. Illinois Crypto Tax Draws Unlikely Alliance of DOJ, Banks and DeFi

Illinois Crypto Tax Draws Unlikely Alliance of DOJ, Banks and DeFi

An unusually broad coalition, spanning the U.S. Justice Department, Wall Street’s biggest lobby, the national Chamber of Commerce and a who’s-who of crypto firms, has thrown its weight behind a legal challenge to Illinois’ first-of-its-kind tax on digital asset transactions.

The groups filed briefs this month in Illinois state court supporting a lawsuit from the Crypto Council for Innovation and the Blockchain Association, which are asking a judge to strike down the state’s Digital Asset Tax Act and permanently block its enforcement.

The tax imposes a 0.2% levy on the full value of a digital asset every time an Illinois customer trades, transfers, or stores it through a broker, even when there’s no sale, gain, or change in ownership.

Critics have called it among the most punitive crypto taxes in the country, in part because Illinois imposes no comparable tax on dollars, gold, stocks or any other asset, and no other state has anything like it.

On Nov. 6, the Justice Department filed a statement of interest arguing the tax undermines federal policy and U.S. leadership in digital assets, calling it vague and discriminatory for singling out crypto for treatment no other asset faces.

The U.S. Chamber of Commerce and the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association, which represents hundreds of broker-dealers, banks and asset managers, filed an amicus brief warning the tax reaches well beyond crypto-native platforms to the traditional banks, custodians and advisers increasingly offering digital asset products.

A separate brief filed Nov. 11 brought together crypto’s heavy hitters, led by the DeFi Education Fund, Andreessen Horowitz, Paradigm and Uniswap, and including Aave Labs, Chainlink Labs, Solana Policy Institute, Phantom, Jito Labs, Multicoin Capital and Stand With Crypto.

Their filing argues the law’s definitions are so vague they could stretch to software developers and decentralized protocols that hold no customer funds and have no way to identify users or know where a transaction occurred. The brief noted an estimated 2.64 million Illinoisans hold digital assets, the fifth-highest total of any state.

“This tax is unclear, discriminatory, unconstitutional, and harmful to the future of finance,” CCI CEO Ji Hun Kim and Blockchain Association executive Ashok Pinto said in a joint statement.

The challenge dates to August. As Decrypt reported, Illinois agreed in October to pause the tax until July 1, 2027, buying time for the court fight now underway.

#5016. Is TRT Causing Your Acne?

Guys who take higher doses of testosterone are at a greater risk of developing acne, but it can happen at any dose.

Treating adult acne can feel like one big science experiment—until now. This is the guy’s guide to adult acne. Read the rest of the stories here.

THERE ARE PLENTY of reasons to like testosterone replacement therapy (TRT)—it can boost energy, increase muscle mass, and improve sex drive—but there’s one potentially annoying side effect: acne, or, uh, bacne (acne on your back and shoulders). TRT can increase sebaceous gland activity, which boosts oil production that can clog pores, trap bacteria, and cause breakouts. The areas around the back and shoulders, along with the chest, have a high concentration of androgen-responsive oil glands.

When the total testosterone in your body increases, it gets converted to dihydro­testosterone (DHT), a major androgen, and estrogen. DHT ramps up oil production in the skin, according to Akhil Muthigi, MD, a urologist at Houston Methodist Hospital. This can lead to inflammatory acne (papules, pustules, nodules, and cysts) though whiteheads and blackheads are common as well. Dr. Muthigi says that guys who take higher doses of TRT (anything near or over 200 milligrams per week) are at a greater risk of developing acne, but it can happen at any dose. It largely depends on your skin biology (i.e., the amount of androgen receptors on your skin that trigger sebaceous gland growth and, therefore, oil production), as well as genetic factors.

TRT treatments that provide steadier levels (such as daily creams and gels) are less likely to cause acne than other forms that cause spikes (such as injections), says Danny Guo, MD, a cosmetic and surgical dermatologist. He adds that if you start TRT and see acne forming within a few weeks to a few months of taking it, stopping the therapy won’t necessarily eliminate the acne. So it’s best to talk to a dermatologist before bailing on the therapy altogether. Fortunately, TRT-triggered acne is as treatable as any form, with the strategies here proving effective.

#5014. Nvidia, AMD, Broadcom: The AI Stock Winners and Losers After Jensen Huang’s Ousting

Nvidia, AMD, Broadcom: The AI Stock Winners and Losers After Jensen Huang’s Ousting

Wall Street is picking winners and losers from Jensen Huang's departure from Nvidia, and the market is treating the chipmaker's competitors considerably better than companies tied to its business.

Nvidia shares fell 4.59% to $165.75 Thursday afternoon, recovering from an opening price of $151.01 and an intraday low of $148.80.

The rebound softened an initial collapse of more than 14%, but the stock remained firmly in negative territory.

The selloff followed Huang's immediate resignation as CEO, president, and board member after reports of extramarital affairs and allegations involving a suspected Chinese intelligence operative. Nvidia named CFO Colette Kress interim CEO, although the company has not established that corporate secrets were compromised.

The stock was already in trouble before Huang stepped down. Nvidia had lost approximately $1.27 trillion in market capitalization between October 6 and November 6, raising questions about AI spending even before the leadership crisis intensified.

AMD climbed 5.79% to $584.89 after opening at $569.28, making it one of the clearest beneficiaries of Thursday's trading.

The company competes directly with Nvidia in AI accelerators, and uncertainty around Nvidia's leadership could make customers more interested in diversifying their chip suppliers.

Broadcom followed a similar trajectory, gaining 5.65% to $364.73 from an opening price of $356.80.

Unlike AMD, which competes through its own GPU products, Broadcom specializes in custom AI processors and networking technology, offering large customers a different way to reduce their dependence on Nvidia.

Google’s parent company Alphabet also advanced, rising 2.06% to $299.49 after opening at $293.77.

Those alternatives existed before Huang resigned, and Thursday's gains do not establish that Nvidia has lost customers. Investors may simply be pricing in better competitive opportunities if the leadership transition disrupts Nvidia's strategy or customer relationships.

Nvidia-backed CoreWeave suffered a much rougher session, falling 7.49% to $56.22 after opening at $50.91.

Its decline came just one day after the cloud provider surged 24.7% following quarterly revenue growth of 168%, so this may be just as much a correction after an explosive movement as it is a reaction to Huang’s ousting.

In any case, the relationship between the companies helps explain the anxiety among investors. Nvidia invested another $2 billion in CoreWeave in January and has an initial $6.3 billion agreement to purchase unused computing capacity through April 2032, making it an investor, supplier, and customer of the same company.

Any change in Nvidia's investment priorities could therefore affect CoreWeave beyond ordinary chip purchases, although neither company has announced changes to those agreements.

The pressure also reached the semiconductor supply chain. TSMC, which manufactures Nvidia's advanced processors, fell 1.15% to $408.61 after opening at $394.62, while memory supplier Micron lost 2.70%, trading at $789.43 after opening at $769.31.

SK Hynix, another major supplier of high-bandwidth memory used in AI hardware, declined 3% to $137.98 from an opening price of $134.25.

These companies depend on broader chip demand, however, so their losses cannot be attributed entirely to Nvidia's leadership problems.

For investors, the immediate concern is less about whether AI chips will stop selling and more about how Nvidia manages its partnerships, financing commitments, and product roadmap without the executive who built much of that network.

Nvidia's board has said it expects operations and customer commitments to continue without interruption. The company is scheduled to report quarterly earnings on November 17, with Kress now leading the business through its first earnings announcement since Huang's departure.

#5011. White House sends crypto-relevant picks to fill SEC and CFTC seats

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Of the nominees, Nathan Anonick and Matthew Rowland have worked on the Clarity Act, while Kristin Johnson has regularly spoken about digital asset regulations.

The White House nominated four individuals to fill vacant seats at the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, making the first such nominations in roughly a year.

Nathan Anonick of Virginia and Matthew Rowland of the District of Columbia were nominated to be commissioners of the CFTC. Kristin N. Johnson of Michigan and Steven Levine of Maryland were nominated to be members of the SEC.

The nominations are bipartisan — Anonick and Levine are Republicans; Johnson and Rowland are Democrats. The Senate Agriculture Committee will consider the CFTC nominees, while the Senate Banking Committee will consider the SEC nominees.

Anonick currently serves as legislative counsel to Senate Agriculture Committee Chairman John Boozman and worked as a lead negotiator on the Agriculture Committee's portion of the Digital Asset Market Clarity Act. Rowland is a CFTC attorney who has been detailed to the Senate Agriculture Committee and also worked on the Clarity Act and the 2024 Agriculture Committee crypto bill.

Meanwhile, Johnson, a former CFTC commissioner, focused on digital assets, risk management, and market integrity. Johnson has spoken often about crypto and the need for regulations around crypto asset custody, DeFi, and AI.

Levine most recently served as General Counsel and a senior advisor at the White House Council of Economic Advisers, and previously worked at the SEC as counsel and deputy chief of staff to Commissioner Mark Uyeda.

Levine was nominated to succeed Hester Peirce, a vocal advocate for the crypto industry who led the agency's Crypto Task Force and assisted Chairman Paul Atkins' Project Crypto to modernize securities regulation to accommodate crypto innovation and advance the goal of making the U.S. the "crypto capital of the world."

Ji Kim, chief executive of the Crypto Council for Innovation, congratulated the nominees in a post on X, stating that strong, bipartisan representation is essential to developing durable rules that safeguard markets, protect consumers, and support innovation.

"Both the CFTC and SEC will continue to play central and consequential roles in our financial markets, and together these nominees bring a wealth of experience and expertise that will help ensure continued American leadership," Kim wrote.

#5010. Crypto hack losses equal 57 cents per $1 earned onchain: Ark’s Valente

Crypto hackers stole $8.7 billion so far in 2026, surpassing the projected annual onchain revenue generated across the industry, according to ARK’s research director.

Hackers stole 57 cents for every $1 earned onchain by blockchain protocols since 2017, according to Lorenzo Valente, director of digital asset research at ARK Investment Management.

“Security is the bottleneck,” wrote Valente in a Thursday X post, sharing a chart showing that $8.7 billion lost to crypto hacks year to date had already surpassed the $5.6 billion in estimated annual onchain revenue for 2026.

Since 2017, the crypto industry has lost about $26.9 billion to hacks and earned $47 billion in total onchain revenue, he added.

The recent $6.3 billion stolen in the Binance hack alone exceeded Valente’s estimate for full-year 2026 onchain revenue. Value lost to hacks is on track to exceed onchain revenue by year-end for the first time since 2020.

The crypto industry saw a string of high-value hacks this year, including September’s $387 million Bitget hack and the $320 million Liquid Network exploit, as well as April’s $295 million Drift Protocol exploit.

The mounting security exploits are weighing on the institutional appetite for DeFi. In an April research note, JPMorgan analysts identified bridge security as a long-standing challenge for the industry.

Total value locked in decentralized finance (DeFi) has fallen nearly 10% since the Binance exploit, from $85.9 billion on Oct. 29 to $77.4 billion on Thursday, according to DefiLlama.

#5008. Who Is Colette Kress, Jensen Huang’s Successor as Nvidia CEO?

Who Is Colette Kress, Jensen Huang’s Successor as Nvidia CEO?

For the first time in 33 years, someone other than Jensen Huang is running Nvidia. And the person taking over is not an engineer, a chip designer, or a Silicon Valley founder.

Colette Kress, Nvidia's 59-year-old chief financial officer, was named interim CEO Thursday following Huang's abrupt resignation amid reports of extramarital affairs and allegations involving a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.

Kress will remain CFO while the board searches for a permanent replacement, according to Nvidia's regulatory filing. She inherits a company worth trillions of dollars, a shaken investor base, and a management structure built around one man.

Kress joined Nvidia in September 2013, when it was primarily known for graphics cards used in video games. She arrived with nearly 25 years of financial experience across some of America's largest technology companies.

She spent eight years at Texas Instruments before joining Microsoft in 1997. During her 13-year tenure there, she became CFO of the Server and Tools division, overseeing financial strategy for Microsoft's enterprise computing business.

In 2010, she moved to Cisco as senior vice president and CFO of its Business Technology and Operations Finance organization, managing financial planning and business development across the networking giant's operations.

Kress holds a finance degree from the University of Arizona and an MBA from Southern Methodist University, according to her official biography.

Her move to Nvidia came with an unusual endorsement. Her two sons, then enthusiastic gamers, encouraged her to join the company responsible for the graphics chips powering their favorite games, according to Forbes.

Huang personally recruited her, explaining his vision for AI computing on a whiteboard during their discussions, Wired reported. At the time, Nvidia was the smallest company Kress had worked for.

When Kress joined Nvidia, its annual revenue stood at approximately $4.13 billion. By fiscal 2026, that figure had reached $215.9 billion, an increase of more than 50 times.

Huang drove Nvidia's technological strategy, but Kress oversaw the finances supporting its expansion from gaming hardware into data centers and AI infrastructure.

Her tenure included Nvidia's $6.9 billion acquisition of networking specialist Mellanox and an increasingly aggressive strategy of investing in companies that purchase its computing hardware.

Those relationships now include CoreWeave, in which Nvidia invested another $2 billion in January. CoreWeave's rapid growth reflects enormous demand for Nvidia-powered computing, alongside the financial costs of building that infrastructure.

Kress also became Nvidia's principal financial voice to Wall Street, regularly explaining margins, chip demand and revenue forecasts during earnings calls.

Forbes ranked her among America's richest self-made women in 2026, with almost $750 million in Nvidia shares as of Wednesday's close.

Kress takes charge as Nvidia faces questions about the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending. Nvidia had already lost approximately $1.27 trillion in market value from its October peak before the board publicly announced its investigation on Huang’s love affairs.

She must also navigate Washington's restrictions on advanced chip exports to China and scrutiny surrounding Nvidia's commercial relationships. The board has said it has no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security were compromised in the allegations involving Huang.

Her financial expertise cannot entirely replace Huang's technical background or his personal relationships with developers, customers, and governments. Maintaining Nvidia's engineering leadership will require close coordination with the executives responsible for its chips and software.

Kress has promised to build on Huang's legacy, saying that “Nvidia's strength has always come from its people.”

Her first major public test arrives Tuesday, November 17, when Nvidia reports its third-quarter fiscal 2027 earnings. The company has scheduled its results for approximately 1:20 p.m. Pacific, followed by an investor call at 2 p.m.

#5007. TRM Labs launches new service to help police seize stolen crypto in minutes

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

TRM Labs has launched Beacon Concierge, a managed service that enables any verified law enforcement agency to work with exchanges and stablecoin issuers to seize illicit crypto funds.

TRM Labs has launched Beacon Concierge, a managed service that enables any verified law enforcement agency to work with exchanges and stablecoin issuers to interdict and seize illicit cryptocurrency funds on any chain in any asset.

According to TRM's Thursday release, the company pointed out that many law enforcement agencies lack direct relationships or the capacity to package requests correctly and act while funds remain in motion. In large incidents, stolen funds have been split across thousands of transactions within days.

The new service is built on the Beacon Network, which TRM Labs launched in August 2025. The network has more than 200 members covering 80% of crypto trading volume. When flagged funds reach a member, the average time to action is measured in minutes, the press release said.

TRM said Beacon Concierge has already supported 15 cases for 14 agencies in 10 countries, with more than $35 million frozen. More than $200 million has been interdicted with Beacon members to date, the company added.

The company's own investigators assist agencies by tracing funds, preparing evidence packages, flagging addresses in the network, and supporting requests for freeze and seizure. Stablecoin issuers can freeze funds on-chain.

"Criminals move quickly, and the agencies pursuing them need a clear path to the issuers who can act," said Esteban Castaño, CEO and co-founder of TRM Labs. "Beacon Concierge puts our investigators, our tracing and the Beacon Network behind every verified agency, so more cases can move from first lead to issuer action."

TRM cited the Beacon Network's response to the Binance exploit as an illustration of the scale involved.

The network has labeled more than 26,000 addresses across 14 blockchains, traced more than 67,000 transfers and links, including over 1,050 cross-chain swaps. TRM said the network followed funds from 59 origin addresses an average of 10 hops and as far as 71.

Network members flagged 39% of the addresses, while Beacon identified the other 61% automatically from those flags. The network delivered more than 45,000 alerts to participating platforms, the company added.

"The Binance hack shows why cross-chain and multi-asset coordination is more important than ever," TRM said. "No investigator could have followed every branch by hand. Beacon Concierge is built so that any agency, not only those with a large team and an existing issuer relationship, can put that kind of coverage behind a case."

An agency can join the network with a single verification process, TRM said. The company will handle tracing, evidence packaging, flagging, and routing, while prioritizing the cases by severity.

Accelerating developments in AI are enabling attackers to launch more sophisticated cyberattacks at scale, especially as they utilize the technology to find vulnerabilities undetected by humans. DefiLlama data shows $8.78 billion has been lost across 341 crypto hacks and exploits year-to-date, led by a $6.36 billion breach at Binance.

#5005. Your ‘Healthy’ Soda Alternative May Not Be So Healthy for Your Liver

WE'RE LONG PAST having to make a decision between just Coke or Pepsi. You've got a lot of options if you're bored of water—from fizzy nonalcoholic drinks to flavored seltzers. But while a lot of these drinks market themselves as healthy alternatives to sodas, research suggests they could be silently doing a number on your liver health.

If you've done the work to switch to diet sodas or have learned to love prebiotic sodas like Olipop or Poppi, the news can be pretty hard to hear. So it's important to get this out of the way upfront: If you have a "healthy" soda every once in a while, the experts we spoke to say the risk of having liver problems is unlikely. The real danger to your liver is if you’re tossing these back on a daily or near-daily basis. Here's everything you need to know.

THE STUDY PRESENTED at the United European Gastroenterology (UEG) Week 2025 used data from 103,251 people who enrolled in the UK Biobank and didn’t have liver disease at the start of the study. Participants were asked to fill out questionnaires about what they ate and drank throughout the day. The researchers then paid close attention to how much sugar-sweetened and low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages they drank.

The major takeaway from the study is that it pretty much linked any drink that wasn’t water to metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD). You might know it by its former name, nonalcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD). But whatever you call it, it's no joke.

MASLD happens when too much fat builds up in your liver from inputs other than alcohol. It can lead to serious liver scarring or end-stage liver disease. It can also put you at high risk for heart disease and diabetes. According to the American College of Gastroenterology, heart disease is the number one killer of people living with MASLD.

DAILY DRINKING OF nearly 9 ounces of any type of drink that was not water was associated with a significantly higher risk of developing MASLD. That risk jumped 50 percent for sugar-sweetened beverages and was surprisingly higher—60 percent—for those who drank low- or non-sugar-sweetened beverages. (Though some of these prebiotic sodas have more sugar and less fiber than marketing claims might imply).

After a 10-year follow-up, 949 people developed MASLD and 103 died from liver-related causes. Both types of beverages were significantly connected to increases in liver fat.

Replacing nearly 12 ounces of sugary beverages with water lowered MASLD risk by 14.4 percent and by 13.5 percent with low- or non-sugar-sweetened drinks. But if you switched completely from a sugary beverage like soda to a low-sugar beverage like fruit juice, the authors found that this did not help lower a person's risk of developing MASLD.

Exactly which beverages raised risk is hard to say because the authors did not directly state what low- or no-sugar drinks people were drinking. That said, there are lots of sodas that are marketed as being healthier alternatives that are not as good as they claim, according to Danbee Kim, MD, nutrition expert and assistant professor at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School.

“Even beverages marketed as ‘natural’ or ‘health-oriented’ may still fall into this category if they rely on sweeteners instead of sugar,” Kim says. “The key commonality is that they provide sweetness without significant calories. However, they may still affect liver health.”

A FEW THINGS may be going on here. “Low/non-sugar sweetened beverages, although low in calories, may still affect liver health through several pathways,” says Lihe Liu, a graduate student researcher at The First Affiliated Hospital of Soochow University in China and lead study author. “They can alter the gut microbiome, interfere with feelings of fullness, increase preference for sweet foods, and in some cases stimulate insulin secretion.”

When you have something that’s sweet, even if it doesn’t contain much or any sugar, it sets off a chain reaction in your body the same way as if you were to actually have sugar, says Albert Matheny, RD, CSCS, registered dietitian and co-founder of SoHo Strength Lab. “You’re basically tricking your body,” he says. “Your body still reacts as if it has sugar.”

As a result, Matheny says your body produces more insulin (a hormone that helps regulate blood sugar), and encourages the liver to take in more sugar from your bloodstream. This can lead to metabolic disruption.

“Some artificial sweeteners may alter how the body handles glucose and insulin, contributing to insulin resistance, which is a key driver of metabolic dysfunction and fat accumulation in the liver,” says Kim. This process may even mess with your appetite, making you think that you're hungry when you're not.

Another reason has nothing to do with your gut, but with your mind. Kim says we can't discount the possibility that people who drink these low- and non-sugar-sweetened beverages may subconsciously compensate for the “saved” calories by eating more sugary or fatty foods later.

Keep in mind that all this is speculation. The explanation for why and how artificially sweetened beverages are linked to MASLD is still under investigation since Liu says her team didn't look too deep into this.

NOT NECESSARILY. “THEY’RE definitely an improvement over traditional diet sodas,” Matheny says. “That’s not to say that they’re a totally healthy option.”

When given a choice, water reigns supreme. And even then you still don't want to go too overboard.

If you still want to crack open a cranberry fizz prebiotic soda or even an unsweetened iced tea, Matheny says that’s probably fine, too. “In moderation, kind of everything is OK.”

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#5004. Thailand proposes $148K daily cap on stablecoin deposits and withdrawals

Thailand’s SEC would set separate daily caps for stablecoin deposits and withdrawals and require customers to use wallets they own.

Thailand’s securities regulator has proposed capping daily stablecoin deposits and withdrawals at 5 million baht ($148,000) each per customer at regulated crypto operators.

The limit would apply separately to deposits and withdrawals at each operator, Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said in a Thursday announcement. The regulator said the rules aim to curb money laundering, cybercrime and attempts to bypass cross-border money transfer rules.

The draft would bar stablecoin transfers to and from third-party wallets. Operators would have to verify that customers own the accounts or wallets they use for deposits and withdrawals.

Operators would have to comply with the Travel Rule, which requires crypto service providers to collect and share information about transfers, and use blockchain analytics to identify wallets linked to suspicious activity.

The draft would require crypto exchanges to name their market makers and identify the assets for which they provide liquidity. Crypto brokers would be barred from using liquidity providers for stablecoin-baht pairs. Certain off-platform trades would be subject to a 3 million baht minimum.

The draft follows a September consultation in which the SEC proposed stablecoin transfer limits and tighter oversight of crypto operators.

#5003. Why the Path to Quantum Resistance Runs Uphill for Bitcoin Wallet Makers

Why the Path to Quantum Resistance Runs Uphill for Bitcoin Wallet Makers

Bitcoin custody has always involved some degree of risk, but wallet manufacturers like Trezor—which build physical devices designed to keep a user’s private keys secure offline—are preparing for a post-quantum world where new challenges arise.

That’s because SHRINCS, the community’s leading candidate for a post-quantum signature scheme, allows users to make transactions using stateful signatures that require rigid state tracking to prevent funds from being stolen, according to Trezor CTO Tomáš Sušánka.

Proposed late last year by Blockstream Research Director Jonas Nick and cryptographer Mikhail Kudinov, SHRINCS centers on a composite hash-based approach: users can make transactions using either stateful signatures or stateless signatures.

Stateless signatures are quite large—potentially affecting fees, signature speed, and other efficiency metrics—but are safe to reuse, while smaller, more efficient stateful signatures come with serious security risks when reused beyond a particular signing budget.

The stateful component of SHRINCS is made up of a tree of one-time signatures, and if these keys are accidentally used more than once, enough cryptographic information is revealed for someone else to forge that user’s signature and effectively steal funds.

If Bitcoin successfully adopts SHRINCS, maintaining a meticulous count of the wallet’s state will become a critical responsibility for wallet developers—with lots of potential edge-case security risks.

“You’re giving them an option to mess up,” Sušánka said during a recent episode of MARA Foundation TV, in reference to state tracking. “It’s our job to make it in a secure way, right? It shouldn't be the user’s job.”

To mitigate this risk, Trezor is weighing options for how to support secure UX in a post-quantum world. For instance, Sušánka said the company is considering making the heavier, slower stateless signatures the default setting for all users, tucking the riskier stateful option behind an advanced settings wall.

While Sušánka recognizes that SHRINCS may be the conservative cryptographic choice because it relies on the same cryptographic assumptions as SHA-256, he is “kind of torn on the topic because maintaining the state is a burden.”

“For example, you can have two Trezors with the same recovery seed. How do you share the state between those two? You really don’t,” he added.

“That being said, as it is with everything, this is about trade-offs,” Sušánka continued. “The [stateful] signatures are 10 times smaller ... That makes a big difference. So, I just don't want to throw it off the table completely.”

Sušánka went on to share that while many are focused on Bitcoin’s migration path to post-quantum addresses and signatures, hardware wallet devices will also need to be physically updated for full quantum protection. Most signing devices use quantum-vulnerable cryptography for hardware-specific security functions, like firmware and device authentication.

If users want to ensure their devices are secure in a post-quantum world, they will need to use “quantum-ready” devices—that is, devices that use post-quantum cryptography for device and firmware authentication as well.

In anticipation of these needs, the company is already shipping products like Trezor Safe 7 that support post-quantum cryptography for wallet authentication processes. Of course, transactions made on these devices will only be quantum-secure once Bitcoin itself upgrades to post-quantum security.

That said, Sušánka warned developers not to wait too long to give users migration options, as he believes the migration of potentially millions of users will take time.

“People should be ready to make compromises,” Sušánka said. “If we’re all going to be very strict on what can and cannot be done, we’re not going to get anywhere.”

André Beganski is a Senior Content Manager at MARA Foundation, producing content on topics including the intersection of quantum computing and cryptography. He has previously worked for Decrypt.
