#5002. This 4-Week Workout Program Uses Just 1 Dumbbell to Help You Get Stronger

Build muscle and fry fat over this month-long workout plan.

YES, YOU CAN work every muscle in your body with one piece of gear. We love kettlebells, med balls, and resistance bands, but if we had to choose one piece of equipment, it would be a single dumbbell.

The game plan: Three times a week, you’ll grab the simplest tool in the gym and use it to build muscle and banish fat in a super-dense half-hour workout. It’s the most effective way to fry body fat, and it’s the perfect way to train for the holidays. It’s also exactly how Hollywood-based trainer Ben Bruno, C.F.S.C., guides time-strapped A-listers like Justin Timberlake and NBA star Klay Thompson. “They want quick, efficient workouts that hit everything,” Bruno says. “So we need to move fast.”

It doesn’t get more efficient than this light-speed, one-dumbbell workout.

Stand holding a dumbbell at your left shoulder, feet shoulder-width apart, knees slightly bent, glutes squeezed, and core tight. Keeping your chest level, straighten your arm to press the dumbbell overhead (b). Pause, then slowly lower the dumbbell back to your shoulder. That’s 1 rep; do 8 per arm.

Stand holding a dumbbell at your chest. Tighten your core and step back about three feet with your right leg. Keeping your chest up and the dumbbell high, bend your knees and lower your torso until your left thigh is parallel to the floor. Your right knee should be a few inches from the floor. Pause, then push off your right leg and stand back up. That’s 1 rep; do 10 per leg.

Eb says: Squeeze your glutes when you stand up. This will help you avoid lower-back pain when doing lunges.

Set a bench to a 45-degree angle and position yourself so your chest is against it; squeeze your abs and glutes. With your left arm out at shoulder height, hold a dumbbell in your right hand, arm hanging naturally. Squeeze your shoulder blades. This is the start. Keeping your shoulders and hips steady, row the dumbbell to your right hip. Pause, then return to the start.That’s 1 rep; do 10 per arm.

Eb says: This is more than a back move; your glutes and abs should be into it, too. Tighten them up so your torso doesn’t tip down to the side of the weight.

Lie on your back, holding a dumbbell in your left hand, arm straight, directly over your chest. Press your lower back into the floor and lift your legs slightly off the floor; lift your shoulder blades slightly, too. This is the start.Bend at the elbow and shoulder, lowering the dumbbell to your chest without losing torso position. Press back to the start. That’s 1 rep; do 8 per arm.

Eb says: Struggling with the position? Bend your knees to make it easier.

Lie on your back, knees bent and feet flat on the floor. Lift your right foot off the floor and tuck your right knee toward your chest. Tighten your core. Driving your left heel into the ground, push your lower back off the floor; squeeze your left glute as you do this. Pause, then return to the start. That’s 1 rep; do 10 per side.

Set up in pushup position, glutes and abs tight, right hand gripping a dumbbell. Squeeze your shoulder blades. Pull the dumbbell off the floor, rowing it toward your rib cage but keeping your hips and shoulders square. Pause. Return the dumbbell to the start. That’s 1 rep; do 8 per arm.

Eb says: Focus on flexing the oblique on the rowing side as hard as you canto keep your hips level.

Before joining Men's Health, he served as a sports columnist and tech columnist for the New York Daily News.

#5000. Strategy Made $19.5 Billion in Profit. So Why Is MSCI Removing It From Stock Indexes?

Strategy Made $19.5 Billion in Profit. So Why Is MSCI Removing It From Stock Indexes?

Strategy made $19.53 billion in profit last quarter. Yet MSCI, one of the world’s biggest providers of stock indexes, has decided Michael Saylor’s Bitcoin company no longer belongs in some of its major investment benchmarks.

MSCI’s November review lists “STRATEGY A”—the company’s MSTR shares—for deletion from the MSCI USA Index after trading closes November 30. That also removes the stock from broader indexes that include U.S. companies, such as the MSCI World and MSCI All Country World indexes.

That sounds technical, but the distinction matters: MSCI is not removing Strategy from the Nasdaq stock exchange, shutting down its business, or making it sell Bitcoin. It is taking MSTR out of stock lists that investment funds use to decide which companies to own.

Consider an ETF designed to copy the MSCI USA Index. When MSTR disappears from the list, the fund generally has to sell its shares, regardless of whether the fund manager likes Saylor’s strategy.

This is why it’s bad news for Strategy. It means less indirect exposition to investments.

MSCI adopted new rules in October for so-called non-operating companies. The idea is to keep investment vehicles—businesses that mainly hold financial assets—out of indexes meant to track operating companies. Its screening process considers a company’s operating assets and other financial measures.

MSCI’s deletion list does not spell out exactly which tests Strategy failed. The company, however, argues that raising capital, managing its Bitcoin treasury and issuing financial products amount to a genuine operating business.

In an August letter to MSCI, Strategy called the proposal “discriminatory, arbitrary, and misguided.” It argued that MSCI should be a “mirror of the market, not an arbiter of it.”

Strategy also maintains a software business, but Bitcoin now dominates its finances. The disagreement is whether its Bitcoin activity counts as running a company or mainly managing an investment portfolio.

Strategy’s latest quarterly earnings show $19.53 billion in net income. But that does not mean its software customers paid the company anything close to that amount.

Nearly all of its $20.91 billion in reported operating income came from a rise in the accounting value of its Bitcoin. Under rules adopted in 2025, changes in Bitcoin’s market price appear in Strategy’s earnings even when it has not sold the coins. A decline in Bitcoin can produce the opposite result.

Its software division, meanwhile, recorded $125.4 million in quarterly revenue, down 3% from a year earlier. Its $477.2 million in revenue for all of 2025 was slightly below the $486.3 million reported in 2019, before Strategy began buying Bitcoin.

The immediate risk is selling pressure on MSTR, not on Bitcoin itself. Strategy estimated that funds linked to MSCI indexes hold roughly 13 million of its shares, or 3.1% of its outstanding common stock. Not all of that necessarily translates into sudden sales, and Strategy says the amount is manageable.

But Strategy has repeatedly raised money by selling shares and other securities, then used those proceeds to buy Bitcoin. If its stock falls, selling the same number of new shares brings in less cash.

So the company would have to issue more shares to raise the same amount—or buy fewer Bitcoin.

That is why a stock-index decision could eventually matter to the crypto market. The exclusion does not force a Bitcoin sale, and it does not guarantee fewer purchases. It could, however, weaken a key source of funding for one of Bitcoin’s biggest corporate buyers.

#4998. Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO, exits board; shares plunge

Colette Kress will remain Nvidia’s chief financial officer while serving as interim CEO as the board searches for a permanent successor.

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as president and CEO and stepped down from the company’s board on Thursday, effective immediately. The board named Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress interim CEO.

Kress will retain her role as CFO while Nvidia’s board considers internal and external candidates for the permanent CEO role. The board also appointed Harvey C. Jones its first chair and said it expects Nvidia’s operations to proceed without interruption.

Nvidia shares fell more than 13% shortly after the US market opened on Thursday, trading at $151.00 as of 2:31 p.m. UTC, according to Yahoo Finance. Bitcoin (BTC) fell alongside Nvidia shares, dropping as much as 3.5% to a multimonth low.

The shares later pared losses to trade at $165 at 4:24 p.m. UTC, down 5% on the day. The stock was down more than 26% over the previous month.

Kress has served as Nvidia’s CFO since September 2013 and previously held finance positions at Cisco and Microsoft.

Nvidia is scheduled to report its fiscal 2027 third-quarter financial results on Nov. 17.

Huang’s departure comes just four days after Nvidia’s board announced an independent investigation amid reports that the company’s co-founder had been targeted by a foreign intelligence effort.

The board said it had no reason to believe US national security or Nvidia’s confidential information had been compromised, but would examine all available information.

In a separate message to employees that day, Huang acknowledged an extramarital affair and apologized to his family and colleagues. He also pledged to cooperate with the investigation.

The Associated Press identified the woman involved in the affair as Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed. The New York Times reported that US intelligence agencies had concluded she was passing information to Beijing. China has denied targeting Huang.

On Tuesday, Nvidia’s independent directors retained former acting US Attorney Joon H. Kim and law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct the inquiry.

On Wednesday, reports said Nvidia’s board was preparing to remove Huang as CEO.

Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and served as its CEO for more than 33 years, overseeing its transformation from a graphics-chip maker into a leading supplier of AI processors. His departure marks the first CEO transition in the company’s history.

Nvidia’s market capitalization surged from approximately $57.5 billion at the end of 2016 to a peak of $5.5 trillion on Sept. 1, 2026, a nearly 96-fold increase, according to data from Stock Analysis.

Amid Nvidia’s sell-off, Huang’s estimated net worth fell to $140.6 billion as of 3:42 p.m. UTC Thursday from $162.2 billion on Nov. 9.

House lawmakers still want to question Jensen Huang about his contacts with Alice Huang and Nvidia’s safeguards against foreign espionage, Rep. John Moolenaar said Thursday.

“We are in discussions with him right now on a date to come and meet with us,” Moolenaar told reporters, according to Politico.

Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, and fellow committee chairs Rick Crawford and Brian Mast first requested a closed-door briefing in a Nov. 4 letter. They cited concerns about Beijing’s access to sensitive US technology and information and asked Huang to confirm by Nov. 11 whether he could attend on Nov. 18 or to propose another date.

Moolenaar said the lawmakers were prepared to subpoena Huang if they could not agree on a date. He also wants to determine whether Chinese intelligence gained access to sensitive information and examine Huang’s lobbying to ease US restrictions on advanced chip exports to China.

Rep. Gregory Meeks, the top Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, said he was considering forming a subcommittee to investigate the matter when the new Congress convenes in January.

#4997. The Best Travel Backpacks for Women in 2026, Tested by Editors

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

The right travel backpack helps you master the art of packing light, so you aren’t hauling unnecessary weight to Terminal B. It has enough space for your flight essentials—laptop, passport, water bottle, charging cables—and is comfortable enough to strap on for hours. Whether you need an upgrade to complement your new carry-on, are investing in a pack to carry you through your next adventure, or simply looking for a great Christmas gift, here’s what to consider before you shop.

Below, see the best travel backpacks for women to take on your next adventure.

Topo’s Rover Pack is truly multifunctional, and that’s why we’ve dubbed it our best overall travel backpack. A chest strap with a buckle closure would have made this backpack perfect to help distribute some of its weight (40 liters can feel quite heavy to some!), but it does come with a luggage strap you can use to rest it on your carry-on or checked luggage while you’re on your way to your destination.

We also love the hidden pocket in the back to store your most valuable items, like money, your passport, or other documents. To pack the main compartment, you can open the bag up like a clamshell, which makes it easy to store your clothing and other travel essentials in a way that makes the most out of the space you have.

The Borealis was originally designed to be used on the trails, but it’s so spacious and easy to organize that it has gained a reputation as a commuter and travel backpack as well.

The inside also features other small pockets for a phone, documents, and other tiny valuables.

We particularly love that this backpack is designed from a recycled nylon and polyester fabric blend that’s both water-repellent and hard to rip. That’s one of the reasons the bag is so durable—it can truly last decades when cared for. The iconic bungee cord system in the front adds a stylish note that makes this backpack stand apart from other options, but the cords are also functional, too. These can be tightened to hold other essentials, like a water bottle, travel blanket, or jacket.

McMahon’s only complaint about this bag is that there are a lot of straps and clips, which can sometimes get in the way and accidentally snag onto other items, she says. Still, she raves that it makes the perfect bag for a weekend trip at 27 liters.

Another cool feature of this bag is that it’s made with The North Face’s FlexVent system, certified by the American Chiropractic Association. This means the shoulder straps and back panel are designed to contour to the curvature of your spine, so that the bag is easier to hold and there’s less of a chance of you experiencing soreness or fatigue.

As denoted by its name, the Triple-Zip Backpack has three main zipper compartments: the main center compartment, the large front pocket, and an external padded laptop pocket that can fit up to a 16-inch laptop. Each pocket is strategically placed, so you have easy access to your laptop, hydration, and any small essentials without having to dig into the main compartment.

Lululemon’s Tech Canvas material features reflective details and is made from recycled polyester, which is water-repellent, making this backpack a great choice for travel. It also has a trolley-compatible sleeve to slip over your luggage while making your way through the airport.

We love the attention to detail built into the thick padded straps—they sit closer together on the back to evenly distribute the load and have an adjustable sternum strap. Plus, load-lifter straps secure the bag to your upper body and the cushy back panel provides lumbar support.

Hikers or outdoor adventurers will enjoy traveling with Cotopaxi’s Allpa 28L Travel Pack. It’s lightweight, made of rip-proof fabric, and easy to clean. The Allpa is the backpack I traveled with when I trekked through the Amazon in Ecuador for two days. My only gripe with the backpack is that it has so many compartments I lost track of where I put things sometimes. But now that I’ve used it several times, that’s no longer an issue. It’s something first-time users can definitely experience, though.

The Osprey bag is made with an adjustable sternum strap that comes with a whistle (particularly useful for adventurers), and a hip belt that helps evenly distribute the bag’s weight to remove strain on your back and shoulders.

Additionally, whenever you do get tired of carrying this workhorse on your back, you can use either of the convenient carrying handles on its top and left sides. If you’re looking for a backpack that easily fits under your airplane seat, Away’s Active Backpack is a solid choice. It’s also a great option for those that will be taking a backpack on their daily excursions once they arrive at their destination.

I also found it to be super lightweight. I wore it on the subway to go catch my bus to Boston from New York, and I had no complaints. I packed it with quite a few things—my laptop, some full-sized beauty products, and two days of outfits—and I never felt that the bag was so heavy that I had to put it down to rest.

The Away bag features several compartments, but my favorite is the hidden pocket in the back behind the bag’s straps. I tend to get paranoid that my wallet will get swiped, but storing it back there reassured me that even the savviest thief would likely not find it. The backpack comes with a removable longer strap you can use to hold the duffel on a shoulder. Like most of the other backpacks in this guide, it features a ton of pockets, including a zippered water bottle pocket on its left side, a laptop sleeve, an internal mesh pocket, two small zippered compartments in the front of the bag, among several others strategically placed inside the bag. The Terra is designed with a clamshell opening, which makes it relatively easy to pack, and two compression straps in the main compartment designed to hold your contents in place.

Because of its 26-liter capacity, on its own, it’s spacious enough for three to four days of travel, but keep in mind that when it’s packed to the brim, we did find that it was a little tough to get its trolley sleeve over a luggage handle. An extra feature we would have loved to see is a waist strap. It does come with a sternum strap, but a waist strap would make this backpack even more comfortable to carry. This bag does get bonus points for being made from 27 recycled plastic bottles. Sustainability is always a plus.

When shopping for a travel backpack, most brands describe how much it can hold in liters. For longer trips over three days, a larger capacity bag—40 liters or more—is the better choice.

For shorter, weekend-long excursions, you can go for backpacks with a 25- to 30-liter capacity. If you’re using your travel backpack in addition to roller luggage, or as a day bag during your travels, you can go even smaller with 18 to 22 liters. “[Too much] weight can be a bit uncomfortable when traveling around Europe and other places, so I try to stay below 40 liters,” says Tamia Ramsey, a travel expert who has been to over 40 countries and founder of the budget travel blog, Poor in a Private Plane.

Ramsey recommends backpacks with four or more compartments, since they make it easy to organize clothing, electronics, valuables, and other travel essentials. She also finds a pocket in the front particularly useful for storing things you may need on the fly, like a pen.

Other features that can make a travel backpack even more functional are a water bottle holder, a shoe or dirty laundry compartment, and a pocket that isn’t too visible for protecting your wallet, passport, and other valuables from thieves.

Finally, if you want to offset the weight of your bag, you can seek options with sternum and chest straps. These straps typically buckle along the front of your body to redistribute the backpack’s weight and take some of the strain off your back and shoulders.

Travel backpacks can open in a ton of different ways, but some of the most popular options include clamshell, U-shaped, and top-loading designs. A clamshell design opens up like a book, similar to a suitcase. These are easy to pack because they give you a bird’s-eye view of what’s in the bag. Others are top-loading, which means they open up at the top, allowing you to pack from the bottom of the bag up. Finally, backpacks with U-shaped openings are made with a zipper that goes around three sides of the backpack. These provide a wider opening than top-loading backpacks. That allows you to access things quickly without having to lay your bag down on the ground, like you would a backpack with a clamshell opening.

The type of opening you choose depends on your preference, but ones with a clamshell opening are typically the easiest to organize and pack because you get such a great view of what’s inside.

Their intel helped me evaluate the following travel backpacks for women on packability, carry comfort, design, durability, and accessibility.

#4996. 12 Best Bar Carts to Elevate Your Entertaining Game (2026)

Bar carts are not just for someone who likes to host a cocktail soirée. In fact, the predecessor of the bar cart was once a Victorian-era tea trolley. Only when the Prohibition hit did these side tables on wheels find their true calling. Many, many iterations later—think: Alvar Aalto’s iconic tea trolley, which has inspired many picks on this list, and USM’s blessedly minimalist spin on the genre—the beauty of this booze trolley remains that it can completely transform a room or give a useless nook some purpose. Though they’ve fallen out of favor in many modern homes, these rolling wonders make an elegant—and functional—home base for all your mixology essentials, from stemware and decanters to liquor bottles and mixers. They also don’t have to be used to haul booze at all, instead doubling as portable storage for crafts, books, beauty or bathroom supplies, and houseplants.

Employed outside, a stylish bar cart can give a dinner party some polish or emulate a poolside butler. Load it up with a pitcher of lemonade and a basket of sunscreen on top, and extra towels on the bottom and you’ll never have to leave your lounge.

Made of solid and veneered walnut with elegant brass details, the Cotu (from the makers of our favorite bar cabinets) is a delightful midcentury-style bar cart that has a distinctive asymmetrical incline on each shelf. Use the wider edge for housing smaller utensils and glasses, while leaving the shallower end for larger bottles that are less likely to topple over the edge.

Birch Lane’s Reinhart cart speaks to farmhouse design, especially in this punchy shade of blue. Turned legs give the overall look a polished appearance, but scalloped paneling on the top shelf and a fun hue ground the piece in something that’s suitable for more playful design aesthetes.

Think of the Wallace Serving Bar Cart as the anti-bar cart. The wooden design resembles a sleek-looking side table thanks to its grounded base, but underneath, a discreet set of wheels gives you the ability to roll the decor piece anywhere a gin and tonic or negroni needs mixing. Its clandestine silhouette also allows it to double as a shelf for non-drink making goods like magazines, coffee table books, or your favorite potted plants.

While this circular cart might lack ample space for large-scale barware, its Art Deco style gives it an Old Hollywood feel. Placed in an area that gets lots of sunshine, the amber tempered glass shelves diffuse a warm glow, layering in some unexpected coziness during cloudless days.

Business & Pleasure Co.’s Al Fresco bar cart is a throwback to a time of leisure under the Parisian sun. While the cart can be used indoors, we can’t imagine a better place for it than on a terrace surrounded by a garden. As if the charming scalloped trim wasn’t enough, the designated umbrella hole nails the summery vibe for poolside mojitos or curbside lemonade stands.

To find that heirloom quality of Victorian design lore, look no further than Greenrow’s Liona bar cart with Barley twist legs to charm you an dyour guests. The two shelves in a chestnut finish offer ample space for glassware and a floral arrangement, should you want to create a whole spread for summer spritzers.

Here’s a piece of decor with lots of Mad Men energy. With its sloped legs and clean-lined silhouette, this cart has enough midcentury-modern charm to appeal to retro design lovers. Two full-size shelves have ample room for old fashioned and martini ingredients, while a mid-tier half shelf can either support a few extra bottles or a stack of your favorite mixology books.

For studio apartment dwellers or homes where square footage is precious, this miniature cart is slim enough to slip into discreet spaces until happy hour rolls around. Made of sturdy beech wood, rounded edges and a light hue soften the beverage cart’s overall look. We think these elements also make it well-suited for adorning Scandi- or Japandi-centric spaces.

Leather accents, exposed wood grain shelves, and an antiqued gold finish bring a polished look and feel to this spacious bar accessory. Two levels with a generous amount of clearance makes it easy to style, whether you’re packing it full of bottles or prefer a more curated decor approach of sculptural glassware.

This sleek, minimalist bar cart will stay with you for many moves. The chrome steel frame defines the iconic silhouette, and you can choose from nine powder-coated steel panels that come in classic black and white, as well as ultra-bold yellow and emerald.

For Bauhaus style on a budget, the Cullin bar cart is more than ready to serve. The angular silhouette resembles less of a cart and more of a full on booze-fueled vehicle, and feels especially cheerful in green or brick red colorways (white and black are also available). With storage for four wine bottles, you can stock multiple kinds of sippers, from sparkling to organic, to please even the snobbiest of guests.

Sturdy and sustainably sourced, this midcentury beauty from West Elm is made in a Fair Trade facility from FSC-certified kiln-dried eucalyptus wood. The soft honey finish of acacia veneer gives this piece accessibility, while the antique brass details add a touch of flair.

#4995. How to Find Out If You Got the Measles Vaccine as a Kid

The US may lose its measles elimination status after multiple outbreaks in the past two years.

THE US is likely to lose its measles elimination status this week when the Pan-American Health Organization, part of the public health arm of the United Nations, meets to review data from the multiple outbreaks that have occurred nationwide in the past two years.As of early November, there are 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 47 states. This is more than double of cases in 2025—the CDC reported 2,289 measles cases in 45 states. And a far cry from when measles were practically eliminated in the US with a record low of 86 cases two decades ago.

Of all the US cases this year, 96 percent come from people who are unvaccinated. Since most of us got the MMR (measles, mumps, and rubella) vaccine as a kid, it wouldn't hurt to double-check that you're protected against one of the most contagious infectious diseases in the world.

Now, there's no central repository of childhood vaccine records in the United States, says Brian Labus, PhD, MPH, assistant professor of epidemiology and biostatistics at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas. So you'd need to find your copy of your vaccination records or get them from your pediatrician's office—which may or may not still be operating. There are a few other places to do that detective work (more below).

In lieu of that, some people are getting blood tests to check if they have antibodies for some of the diseases children are vaccinated for, but there are pros and cons with those. Here's what to know about making sure you're protected against measles and whether you got other vaccinations on the childhood immunization schedule.

IF YOU WERE born before 1957, it’s assumed that you were exposed to measles as a child and don’t need to be vaccinated, Labus says. If you’re around 45 or older, you were likely exposed to chicken pox and don’t need that vaccine, adds Thomas Russo, MD, chief of the division of infectious diseases at the University at Buffalo. There aren’t specific age cutoffs for other diseases, though.

But there is a blood test, called an antibody serology test, that can tell you if you have antibodies against certain diseases, including measles, mumps, chicken pox, hepatitis, tetanus, HIV, mononucleosis, and others.

These blood tests can suggest whether you’ve been previously infected with these diseases or vaccinated against them, Dr. Russo says. “The presence of antibodies indicates that your body mounted an immune response against a pathogen either due to a natural exposure or to a vaccination,” Labus explains. “It can also give you an idea about the level of protection you have in your body.”

While that generally means that you’re immune, it’s also not a guarantee, says Georges Benjamin, MD, executive director of the American Public Health Association. Also, antibodies decline over time.

Starting with an antibody test to determine your vaccination status might make sense for some people, but Labus says there are some practicalities to consider.

For one, the tests can be expensive, costing around $250, Dr. Benjamin says. Your insurance may or may not cover them.

It can also take several days to get the results. Labus says if you’re using the test to determine if you need a vaccine quickly, you’re potentially not protected during that wait. “While this doesn't matter for routine situations, it could be the difference between getting measles or avoiding it if you live in a community experiencing an outbreak,” he says.

If you're not sure you were vaccinated, Labus and the other doctors we spoke to suggest just getting the MMR, polio, DTap (diphtheria, tetanus, and acellular pertussis), and other shots just to be safe (more about that below).

But if you're not sure you want to get jabbed, your pediatrician retired long ago, and you have a moment to invest in detective work on your records, here's where to look:

• Old records you or your parents have at home, like a baby book.

• Any schools you attended. Many states require certain vaccines before you start kindergarten, including MMR, polio, DTap, and varicella, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. If you were in the military, you would have been required to get certain vaccines.

• The health department where you grew up. Some state health departments keep immunization records, Dr. Benjamin says. But whether yours are there may depend on your age. “State immunization registries are a relatively new development, and those systems were not likely in place when you were young if you are more than 25 or 30 years old,” Labus says.

TALK TO YOUR doctor about your concerns and uncertainty about your childhood vaccination status and level of protection against diseases, and they can suggest whether just getting the vaccine for the first time or a booster is appropriate, Labus says. “Most vaccines can be given to adults without any problems.”

Getting the shot is usually cheaper and faster than an antibody test, he adds. Dr. Benjamin points out that boosters are a good idea to consider especially if you have chronic diseases, immune system dysfunction, or work in the healthcare industry. Also consider it if you plan to travel to parts of the world with higher instances of diseases like measles, such as countries in the Middle East, Dr. Russo adds.

And by the way, even if you did get all your vaccines as a child, you still need to get a DTap or Tdap booster every 10 years as an adult for protection against diphtheria and tetanus, Dr. Russo says.

For the other childhood vaccines, “receiving an extra dose is going to boost your antibody levels even if you have previously been vaccinated, which is a good thing,” Labus explains. Dr. Russo agrees: “The path of least resistance is when in doubt, there’s no harm in getting a booster shot.”

#4994. North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korean hackers have stolen more than $400 million in cryptocurrency over the past 18 months using a social-engineering scheme built on secretly recorded footage and AI deepfakes, the U.S. Justice Department said Thursday, as it announced the seizure of the infrastructure behind the operation.

The scheme, which the FBI calls “FaceStealer,” turns video conference calls into a trap. According to the DOJ, hackers lure targets into meetings using accounts stolen from previous victims, then play prerecorded webcam footage of trusted contacts to simulate their presence and win the target’s confidence.

The group also generates deepfake videos of well-known figures in the crypto and related industries, using AI to make the ruse more convincing.

Once a target is at ease, the attackers manufacture a fake technical glitch, such as claiming the person can’t be heard, and offer a link to “fix” it, which instead installs malware.

From there, the hackers can drain crypto wallets or exploit the victim’s access to other systems, sometimes waiting months or more than a year before striking. The FBI said it seized over 900 webcam recordings of FaceStealer victims under a federal warrant.

The DOJ tied the operation to North Korean groups known variously as Sapphire Sleet, BlueNoroff and APT38, among others, and warned the hackers have increasingly targeted open-source software developers in an apparent bid to enable supply-chain attacks.

The announcement came paired with an update on the record-breaking Binance hack. The department said it has now obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of the roughly $6.4 billion stolen from the exchange on Oct. 29, part of over $2.7 billion frozen so far.

The FBI attributed that theft to the North Korean group TraderTraitor, and the regime has been laundering the proceeds at speed, spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds. North Korea has denied involvement, calling the FBI’s finding a political provocation.

The actions underscore how central crypto theft has become to Pyongyang’s finances. The U.S. recently sanctioned 11 people over a North Korean IT-worker scheme that moved millions in crypto, and researchers have cataloged the many ways the regime’s hackers break into crypto firms.

Officials said efforts to trace and recover the stolen Binance funds are ongoing.

#4992. This Guy Lost 54 Pounds and Got Absolutely Shredded in 6 Months

At 46, Austin Cowburn wanted to overcome injuries and get back to feeling his best. Here’s how he did it.

At age 46, Austin Cowburn felt his training was "directionless," and he started putting on weight. This former runner and marathoner wanted to overcome injuries and get back to feeling his best. Here’s how he did it.

RUNNING HAS ALWAYS been my go-to exercise; as a younger man I joined several running clubs, and even finished as even finished pretty high up in the 1995 New York Marathon. I spent weeknights and Saturday mornings on the track or in a swimming pool. Cardio is familiar to me, and as I grew up, I stuck with it. Looking back, I realize means I didn’t have the understanding and technique to really benefit from weight training.

After a couple of years of directionless training, plus two injuries and rehab, and a poor diet, I put on weight. That inflamed other, niggling injuries, which made me feel like I just wasn’t at my best. I felt sluggish; my favorite clothes didn’t fit as well, and the new ones seemed to be smaller than I expected. I wanted to get back into marathons, but to do that I need to get fitter, stronger, and healthier. So, I approached Ultimate Performance Manchester for help.

We started with fundamental stuff: monitoring and being consistent with my diet. I went from eating a lot of rich, calorie-laden food, eating it at all times of the day, to something much more sustainable. I’d enjoy a blended berry smoothie in the morning with protein, and three scrambled eggs with spinach in mid-morning. Mid-afternoon and evening, I’d eat lean-meat chili, mainly turkey mince, with lots of vegetables thrown in, including peppers and tomatoes, as well as rice. I also enjoyed a low-fat, high-protein yogurt in the evening mixed with banana, honey, or chia seeds. On the weekend, I could indulge in a nice steak.

We often forget how big an impact diet can have on mood, energy levels, and your ability to train hard and get the best from yourself. Changing what I ate really reminded me of the importance of a foundational diet.

I trained three times a week in the gym, usually from 8 a.m. to 9 a.m. I loved getting it done first thing in the morning, which really set me up for the day. I would also run four times a week. I really got my love for running back during this time: my distances increased, and I had no injuries. I didn’t even have aches and pains afterward. Training, losing weight, and fueling my body correctly really helped me regain my fluidity of motion.

It also helped with my energy levels and sleep. I felt mentally sharper, more productive and more creative. People told me that my skin was glowing. Everyone was extremely positive and kind in and out of the gym, complimentary, and really encouraging. My family was very proud and surprised with the results, and I felt like the very best version of myself.

In six months, I lost 24.6 kg (54 pounds), bringing my body fat percentage from 28.2% to 11.6%. I dropped from an extra-large to a medium. I’ve continued training, and now I’m eating more, and building muscle mass. I’m looking to sustain my gains and also increase the number of exercises I have in my toolbox.

It’s great to be able to say that more, better training has helped me avoid injuries. The more I trained, the more I wanted to, and my stamina has gone through the roof. I’m happier, more relaxed, and more motivated; I just feel better about myself.

I think anyone can do this, and if you’re on the fence about it, just go for it. Set clear goals and look for help, whether that’s a professional trainer or just a friend who’ll hold you accountable. You don’t have to change all at once; I’d recommend not putting too much pressure on yourself from session to session. Enjoy your exercises; make sure you rest and refuel correctly.

#4989. DTCC Plans Solana Expansion as Wallet Rules Limit Access

The US securities clearinghouse plans to bring tokenized stocks to Solana in 2027, but analysts say restrictions could limit adoption.

Plans by the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation’s (DTCC) to bring tokenized securities to Solana could attract more traditional financial institutions to the blockchain, but regulatory restrictions may limit the impact on the network, according to analysts.

DTCC announced Thursday that it plans to connect its tokenization service to Solana in the second half of 2027. The move would allow certain securities held by its Depository Trust Company (DTC) subsidiary to be turned into digital tokens on the network.

Solana is a blockchain known for its fast transactions and low fees, making it popular for crypto trading, especially memecoins. The network had about $4.94 billion in total value locked (TVL) as of Nov. 12, according to DefiLlama.

Experts say the move could help Solana attract more activity beyond just memecoins, but it’s not clear how much new capital and transaction volume it could bring to the network. What’s more, current US regulatory rules still limit who can hold and transfer the tokenized assets.

Alex Lin, founding partner at venture capital firm Reforge, said Solana’s history of handling large amounts of memecoin trading may have helped show that the network can support traditional financial activity.

“Memes were a public stress test that gave Solana credibility that it can handle retail-scale volume cheaply and reliably,” Lin told Cointelegraph. “The casino paved the way for more institutional plumbing.”

However, under a December 2025 SEC no-action letter, DTC participants must register wallets to hold tokenized securities. The tokens can only be transferred between registered wallets, potentially limiting usage.

“It’s overstated in the near term because the SEC relief from Dec 2025 keeps these tokens in a walled garden of registered DTC participants, so permissionless retail and DeFi liquidity can’t touch these tokens directly yet,” Lin said. “Regulators need to let these assets work as true onchain collateral and that’s more policy than tech.”

DTCC launched its tokenization service on the Canton Network and its private Besu network on Oct. 28, allowing certain securities to be turned into digital tokens while keeping the same investor protections. At launch, DTCC said 20 DTC participants had signed agreements to access the service, but it has not said whether any plan to use Solana.

Cointelegraph contacted DTCC and the Solana Foundation for additional comments but has not yet heard back.

Related: Robinhood’s Tenev warns US tokenization uncertainty deters ‘serious assets’

Kha Nguyen, CEO and co-founder of blockchain analytics platform Birdeye, which tracks trading activity on Solana, said the blockchain has already seen growing demand for tokenized stocks without DTCC’s involvement.

According to Birdeye’s H1 2026 report, tokenized-stock trading volume on Solana reached around $4.9 billion in the first half of the year, more than six times the volume recorded in the previous six months.

Birdeye’s third-quarter report also found that more than 1 million accounts held tokenized stocks on Solana by late September.

Still, broader trading activity on Solana has slowed with the network recording about 6.9 billion transactions in the 30 days ending Nov. 11, down almost 40% from the previous 30-day period, according to Token Terminal data. Meanwhile, trading volume on decentralized exchanges (DEXs) fell 34.4% to $39.1 billion, while monthly active addresses dropped nearly 13% to 45.8 million.

Network fees also fell 39% to about $17 million over the same period. However, the value of tokenized real-world assets (RWAs) on Solana rose almost 7% to $4.76 billion as of Nov. 11 – up from $4.46 billion on Oct. 12.

“By late September more than a million wallets had held a tokenized stock, and real-world assets on Solana had grown five times in a year,” Nguyen told Cointelegraph.

Nguyen said bringing more tokenized securities onto the network does not automatically mean more transactions or greater demand for Solana’s native cryptocurrency, SOL. “A trade is one transaction whether it moves $100 or $100 million,” Nguyen said. “What matters is turnover, not value parked on-chain.”

Nguyen said DTCC’s integration would need support from banks and other financial firms to have a real impact on Solana. Institutions would also need enough liquidity to support large trades.

“Banks, brokers and issuers need to say they will issue, trade and settle there, and then do it,” Nguyen said. He added that institutions would need to actively use the tokenized securities rather than simply hold them.

“The assets must be active, not idle: traded around the clock, used as collateral to borrow against, and earning yield,” Nguyen said.

Lin agreed that the longer-term impact would depend on how the assets are used, rather than which blockchain they are issued on.

“No one cares where the assets are issued, but more so where they’re used,” Lin said. “It’s all about where secondary trading and collateral activity will happen and ultimately settle.”

#4988. Republican Senator Wants to Freeze AI Data Center Boom as Congress Pushes to Expand It

Republican Senator Wants to Freeze AI Data Center Boom as Congress Pushes to Expand It

Republican Senator Josh Hawley wants Congress to hit the brakes on new data centers, just as lawmakers are moving forward with legislation intended to make building the infrastructure behind America's AI boom easier.

The Missouri senator called for a nationwide moratorium Wednesday, listing it among five priorities for Senate Republicans during the six weeks before Democrats take control of the chamber.

“Senate Republicans trying to figure out what to do in the next 6 weeks?” Hawley wrote on X. His answer included a moratorium on data centers, alongside defunding Planned Parenthood, suspending the gas tax and other Republican priorities.

A spokesperson told Politico that Hawley wants a federal pause until Congress passes a “bill of rights giving taxpayers guarantees of no rate increases.” Hawley has not presented detailed legislation specifying which projects would stop or how long the freeze would last.

Data centers are the warehouses packed with powerful computers that train and operate services such as ChatGPT. Building more AI systems means installing more servers, which requires additional electricity, cooling equipment and often upgrades to local power grids.

Senate Republicans trying to figure out what to do in the next 6 weeks? Here you go:DEFUND Planned ParenthoodMORATORIUM on data centersSUSPEND the gas tax FUND rural hospitals SAVE AMERICA ACT

— Josh Hawley (@HawleyMO) November 11, 2026

The scale helps explain the concern. U.S. data centers consumed an estimated 183 terawatt-hours of electricity in 2024, according to figures cited by Pew, and that could climb to 426 by 2030. In other words, their annual electricity demand could more than double in six years.

On Monday, the Senate voted 73-25 to advance debate on the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, a proposal backed by lawmakers from both parties and President Donald Trump's administration.

The bill would speed federal reviews for energy projects and transmission lines, helping bring new power supplies online. It would also require data center operators to pay the additional grid costs their projects create, rather than shifting those expenses onto ordinary electricity customers.

In practical terms, Congress is considering how to keep construction moving without making households subsidize it. Hawley is asking lawmakers to stop new construction until consumer protections are in place.

His position is not entirely new. In February, Hawley introduced the GRID Act with Democratic Senator Richard Blumenthal, seeking to make new data centers obtain electricity from their own sources rather than relying on existing grid supplies.

He now joins independent Senator Bernie Sanders and Democratic Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez in publicly supporting a national pause. Their moratorium proposal goes further, calling for rules addressing AI's effects on workers and public safety as well as the environment.

The politics have shifted quickly. In the November 3 elections, voters backed restrictions on data center development across the country, including local bans and measures requiring public approval before construction.

A Pew Research Center analysis published Wednesday found 60% of Americans uncomfortable with a new data center in their area, including 54% of Republicans and 68% of Democrats. Another survey found 58% disliked political leaders who support building data centers near them.

Opponents worry about rising bills, water consumption and noise. Cities have already imposed restrictions, while OpenAI recently promised to cover electricity and grid costs at future campuses it owns and operates.

Industry supporters see a bigger economic risk. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink warned last month that a construction moratorium could trigger a recession because so much investment now depends on AI infrastructure.

A federal construction freeze could delay the additional computing capacity that AI companies expect to bring online, along with the power infrastructure built to support it. Existing data centers would not necessarily shut down, but the scope of Hawley's proposed moratorium remains undefined.

Hawley's call does not freeze a single project, and he has not said whether he would support the Sanders bill. For now, the Senate is debating legislation to speed infrastructure approvals, not legislation to ban new data centers.

#4986. US Justice Department, business groups and DeFi firms challenge Illinois crypto tax

Industry groups argued in court filings that Illinois’ crypto tax, which applies even when a transaction yields no profit, could raise compliance costs and violate federal law.

The US Department of Justice, business and financial industry groups and a coalition of decentralized finance (DeFi) firms have backed a legal challenge to Illinois’ 0.2% digital asset tax, arguing that the measure unfairly targets cryptocurrency transactions and could impose taxes even when investors make no profit.

Over the past week, the Justice Department filed a statement of interest, while the US Chamber of Commerce and the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association (SIFMA) submitted an amicus filing. A coalition that includes venture capital firms Andreessen Horowitz and Paradigm as well as crypto protocols Uniswap and Aave Labs also filed a brief, according to a Thursday announcement from the Blockchain Association and Crypto Council for Innovation.

The law, enacted in June, imposes a 0.2% tax on the value of digital assets exchanged, transferred or stored, regardless of whether a transaction generates a profit.

The Digital Chamber sued Illinois Attorney General Kwame Raoul and state revenue official David Harris in July, followed by a separate challenge from the Blockchain Association and Crypto Council for Innovation in August. Originally scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, the tax was delayed until July 1, 2027, allowing more time for the legal challenges.

Illinois had projected the digital asset tax would generate $60 million in fiscal year 2027 revenue, according to estimates from the governor’s budget office. Illinois House Speaker Emanuel “Chris” Welch previously promoted the measure as part of a broader affordability agenda aimed at “closing corporate loopholes and making big tech companies and crypto billionaires pay their fair share,” the Chicago Tribune reported in July.

Lawmakers involved in advancing the legislation and the governor’s office did not respond to Cointelegraph’s requests for comment before publication.

Related: France advances stablecoin tax, eyes self-custodial wallets and exit tax proposals

In a Nov. 6 court filing, SIFMA and the US Chamber of Commerce warned that Illinois’ tax would affect banks, broker-dealers, custodians and asset managers across the country, potentially exposing them to significant compliance costs. They also highlighted the financial burden on customers.

“This means that a customer who loses money owes the same tax as a customer who earns a profit,” the filing said. “And a customer who moves the same asset between two accounts faces tax (potentially on both ends) without a sale, a gain, or a change in beneficial ownership.”

The groups said firms would need to develop systems to identify Illinois customers, value digital assets and determine which transactions are taxable, while facing potential criminal penalties for violations.

They also argued that the tax discriminates against digital asset services because comparable transactions involving traditional assets are not taxed in the same way, potentially violating federal internet tax protections and constitutional requirements for interstate commerce and uniform taxation.

The Justice Department also weighed in on Nov. 6, saying that Illinois’ digital asset tax undermines federal efforts to promote cryptocurrency innovation and US leadership in the sector.

The filing cited the creation of a Strategic Bitcoin Reserve and the GENIUS Act, which established federal rules for stablecoins. The DOJ argued that less favorable tax treatment for digital assets could conflict with those policies.

Separately, the DeFi coalition warned about who could be held responsible for collecting the tax. In a Nov. 11 filing, the group argued that Illinois’ proposed rules could impose obligations on software developers and other blockchain participants who lack customer information, control over user funds or the ability to collect taxes.

Before diving into the most intimate of manscaping, it’s important to consider a few key components of the device you’ll be using, given the sensitivity of the region. Not only to avoid any dreaded nicks or cuts, but also to ensure that the process is completed as seamlessly as possible.

Whenever you’re dealing with a trimmer of any kind, the battery life is one of the most prominent considerations. No one wants their device to sputter out and die when they’ve only managed to trim half their beard, and the same is true for the nether regions. Sure, it may be less noticeable than having half a beard, but it can still be uncomfortable. Look for devices that can hold a charge for at least an hour; this should guarantee you two to three sessions per charge.

Any less and you’re going to have to really be diligent with charging after every use or run the risk of it dying out before the next job’s done. The easiest way to avoid this scenario is to look for a rechargeable device, but more on that in a sec.

If you’re a committed pubic trimmer and never travel without one, look for the lighter options to ensure you can comfortably keep them in a carry-on or bag without weighing yourself down. While the TSA and airlines do not generally have a weight limit specifically for grooming devices, they do limit the weight of your bags, so we recommend sticking to trimmers that are under 10 oz to avoid too much bulk. Plus, if you’re new to this type of thing, steering away from tools that are heavier than this until you get a better idea of the process will help you avoid any painful slip-ups.

Similarly, how often do you change up your overall manscaping look? If you find yourself often changing up lengths and styles, you’re going to want to grab a device with multiple attachments and length options. The Braun Series 9 is the best-case scenario here, as it comes packed with nine attachments that create a total of 40 (!) length options. On the other hand, if you typically go full bald or keep it one consistent length, focus on the device’s head shape and blade material or close shaving ability rather than the number of attachments.

For the closest shave (outside of a cartridge razor), look to stainless steel or metal blades, as these can handle the most motor power and are more adept at plowing through thicker hair. While most of the trimmers on this list (and pubic trimmers in general) do not list their official motor power, having stainless steel blades is often the best indicator of a powerful tool, as it shows the brand looks towards the highest quality materials and performance available.

Not to mention, stainless steel blades are typically easier to clean. That being said, the risk of nicks and cuts is higher, so if you’re new to pubic trimming, opt for ceramic or plastic blades. These are gentler on skin, though they won’t get you as close to bare skin.

Use a rechargeable pubic trimmer to avoid mid-trim shutdowns and eliminate the need for spare batteries. Keep it charged between 30 percent and 80 percent to maintain performance and prevent it from dying when you need it most.

Do you prefer to do your manscaping in or outside of the shower? There is no wrong answer, but whichever you prefer is worth keeping in mind when choosing a trimmer. Some are made to be used wet, like the Philips Norelco OneBlade, while others not so much. If you’re a dedicated shower groomer, focus on the fully waterproof options.

As Men’s Health’s grooming editor, I test electric razors and similar devices for a living. When it comes to pubic trimmers, we’ve been testing these devices for nearly three years since this story was first published in 2023. For this update, I tested or retested over 15 pubic trimmers and razors, while consulting with skin and haircare experts on which tools they recommend in 2026.

I also considered top-reviewed trimmers that had high customer ratings on trusted e-commerce websites. After putting each device to the test, I evaluated its effectiveness, safety, and value. Following 12 months of testing, these seven tools proved to be the best for the job.

1. First and foremost, make sure you have ample time and are not rushed. This is not something you want to speed through.

2. Position yourself in front of a mirror with good lighting to make sure you have good visibility for all the crevices.

3. Grab your trimmer and pick your desired length. If you plan to fully shave your pubic hair, set the length long enough to shave easily but not too long—about a couple of days’ worth of stubble. If you’re trimming, start with a longer setting than you want at first; you can always trim it shorter later.

4. Apply baby powder on your pubic area while it’s dry to help soak up extra moisture and plump up the hair.

5. With short, gentle strokes, use the trimmer to cut the hair. Pubic hair grows in a variety of directions, so you’ll want to go slow and possibly go over the same areas a few times. For your scrotum and shaft, go extra slow and stretch the skin to flatten it and make it easier to trim. If you’re trimming your buttcheeks, crack, or perineum, you may want to place a hand mirror on the floor to help see where you’re trimming. You can also place one leg on a tub or toilet to make the area easier to see.

6. If you’re happy with the trim job, then you’re done! Hop in the shower to wash off.

7. If you’re going to shave completely, once you’re out of the shower, apply a pre-shave oil and shaving cream to the areas you’re going to shave. The key here is not to be stingy with the cream.

8. When you’re taking a razor to your nether regions, you’ll want to be extra careful. Shave with the grain of the hair in short, slow strokes. Do not—I repeat—do not rush. You’ll want to use the same stretching technique on your scrotum and shaft as you did while trimming, and the mirror is ideal if you’re taking the razor around back.

9. Make sure to rinse the razor frequently to avoid clogs and reapply shaving cream as needed.

10. Once you’re done, hop back in the shower to rinse everything away.

11. Apply a moisturizer or body oil to the area to help keep it protected. You may also want to apply a moisturizer or anti-chafe product regularly (like at least once a day) to help reduce friction, which can cause irritation on shaved skin, especially below the belt.

Maybe you’re a regular manscaper, or perhaps you just took the plunge into trimming out your nether regions. Regardless, one truth holds true: As soon as you take off the hair, it blooms right back again.

Sure, it might seem that your pubic hair is on a steroid-fueled regrowth. But that’s actually something of an optic illusion, says Dr. Alexes Hazen, MD, a reconstructive and cosmetic plastic surgeon at NYU Langone Health.

People who trim or shave their pubic hair often believe it grows faster than the hair on their head, but that’s a misconception, she says. We merely notice hair growth more and its speed when it’s shorter.

With pubic hair—and other body hair—the entire process takes about 30 to 44 days, Dr. Hazen says. That means the same pubes that started to grow will shed in about a month to a month and a half.

#4982. Strategy stock sheds 5% amid MSCI index removal, Bitcoin sell-off

Strategy shares declined Thursday following MSCI’s decision to remove the company from its indexes, as Bitcoin also fell amid a broader crypto market decline.

Shares of Michael Saylor’s Bitcoin treasury company Strategy fell 6.49% on Thursday, closing at $114.41, a day after MSCI confirmed the company’s removal from its global equity indexes and amid a broader cryptocurrency market sell-off.

On Wednesday, MSCI confirmed that Strategy would be removed from its global equity indexes after the Nov. 30 market close, a move that could trigger selling by funds tracking the benchmarks. JPMorgan previously estimated that the exclusion could result in roughly $2.8 billion in selling pressure.

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, told Cointelegraph that MSCI’s decision could help distinguish Bitcoin treasury companies from traditional operating businesses and limit indirect cryptocurrency exposure for passive investors.

“Index inclusion meant ordinary passive investors, like someone holding a global index fund in their pension, were getting leveraged Bitcoin exposure they never chose. MSCI just fixed that,” he said.

Kirkley added that MSCI’s decision also raises broader questions about whether Bitcoin treasury companies should be classified alongside traditional operating businesses.

In its Aug. 31 response to MSCI’s proposed rules, Strategy argued that the changes were a pretext for targeting digital asset treasury companies. It also downplayed the potential financial impact, saying MSCI-linked funds held approximately 13 million of its shares, representing just 3.1% of its basic shares outstanding.

The stock’s decline coincided with a broader cryptocurrency market sell-off. Bitcoin traded near $71,340 as of 4:08 p.m. ET on Thursday, down 4.3% over the past 24 hours, according to CoinGecko data. The total cryptocurrency market capitalization declined 3% to around $2.49 trillion.

Think about it: excellent underwear isn't just about looking cool (though that's like icing on the cake). It's about how it performs in real life. Are you lounging at home? Going for a long run? Stuck on a flight for hours? Each of these scenarios demands something different. The best breathable underwear manages heat and moisture so sweat can escape instead of getting trapped against your skin. Many use micro-knit or mesh-like weaves that create tiny air channels within the fabric, allowing heat to dissipate and air to circulate as you move. Others incorporate moisture-wicking fibers to pull sweat away from the body and spread it across the surface of the fabric so it evaporates faster. Some brands even add targeted “cool zones” or thinner panels in high-sweat areas to boost airflow where you need it most.

When underwear can’t breathe, moisture builds up, leading to overheating, chafing, irritation, and odor. Breathable underwear works with your body by keeping things cooler, drier, and more comfortable throughout the day. For Men’s Health guys, breathability makes a difference for everyday wear, long commutes, desk jobs, workouts, travel days, and humid conditions. It’s especially clutch for guys who sweat easily, live in hot climates, train hard, or spend long stretches in the same pair from morning to night.

It depends on personal preference, comfort, and the activity. Here's a quick breakdown to help guide your choice, weighing the pros and cons of both.

Tight underwear (e.g., briefs, boxer briefs) provides better support, which is especially useful for physical activities like running or working out. This type reduces chafing and prevents excessive movement, which can help with comfort throughout the day. Works well under slim-fit pants, as it avoids bunching and creates a cleaner silhouette. If it's too tight, it can restrict airflow and potentially cause discomfort or sweating, especially in warmer weather. Overly tight fits might not be ideal for guys concerned about reproductive health, though the effect is typically negligible with properly sized underwear.

On the other hand, loose underwear is better suited for lounging or casual, low-impact activities since it allows more airflow and freedom of movement. It feels more breathable, making it ideal for hotter climates or relaxed settings. Also, loose underwear can be paired with loose-fit pants for added comfort. This option lacks support, which feel uncomfortable during workouts or physical activity. Plus, it can cramp your style—bunching up under tighter pants and offering a restrictive feel.

If you want to protect the structural integrity of your underwear's waistband, the best way to keep them from stretching is to hand wash them and hang them dry. But given how impractical that is, the second best option is to put them in the washing machine on cold water and use a low dryer setting so the elastic doesn't warp. However, our testers say that most of the pairs they've tried have kept their waistbands' shapes even after several trips to the laundromat. If your underwear's waistband has lost its shape, you're probably overdue to replace it.

Even the best underwear won’t last if you don’t take care of it properly. Washing underwear inside out helps remove sweat and bacteria more effectively while protecting the fabric’s surface. Cold or warm water is usually best, as hot water can break down elastic fibers and cause shrinkage over time. Skip harsh detergents and fabric softeners, especially for performance or moisture-wicking underwear. Softeners can coat fibers and reduce breathability and odor control. When possible, air-drying is ideal, as high heat from dryers is the fastest way to kill stretch and warp waistbands. If you do use a dryer, stick to low heat.

#4978. Kalshi ends Donald Trump Jr. advisory role over Polymarket investment: Reuters

The prediction market operator said the decision was mutual and followed 1789 Capital’s investment in rival Polymarket, denying political considerations.

Prediction market platform Kalshi has ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. after his venture capital firm increased its investment in rival Polymarket, Reuters reported Thursday.

A Kalshi spokesperson told Reuters that talks about ending Trump Jr.’s advisory role began in August, after Trump Jr.’s venture capital firm, 1789 Capital, took the lead on Polymarket’s $1 billion funding round. The round would value the company at $21 billion, with 1789 Capital set to contribute about $300 million.

“We have mutually agreed to end Don’s advisory role after his firm’s significant investment in a competitor this past summer,” the spokesperson said.

Trump Jr. joined Kalshi as a strategic adviser in January 2025 and received an equity stake reportedly worth $300,000 at the time, according to the Financial Times.

Kalshi’s spokesperson said political considerations played no role in the decision. Two people familiar with the matter told Reuters, however, that some within Kalshi had raised concerns about what Democrats’ midterm sweep of both chambers of Congress could mean for the company.

#4977. Everyone Is Capable of Having Multiple Orgasms. Sex Experts Explain How

Plus, they share the health benefits of an extended climax.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Pleasure powerfully contributes to overall well-being. If your sex life feels good, it's likely that you feel good, too—emotionally, mentally, physically, and relationally. That's why, especially for women and people with vulvas, having multiple orgasms can be an awesome way to amplify your life in—and out—of the bedroom.

Simply, “multiple orgasms refer to a person having more than one orgasm in a single session, with short or no recovery time in between,” says Shamyra Howard, PhD, CST, LCSW, a sex therapist and author of Use Your Mouth. Just to be clear: “There’s no set or universal number of orgasms a person can achieve in one setting,” she adds. “Some people may experience two or three; others five (or more) in a single session.”

For those with vulvas, multiple orgasms might begin with one climax that immediately blends into the next with little or no notable pause. Sometimes called rolling orgasms, these “can feel like riding wave after wave of feel-good sensation,” says sex and relationship expert Tara Suwinyattichaiporn, PhD, a professor of relational and sexual communication at California State University and author of How Do You Like It?.

Other folks may have a brief comedown period followed by a slower climb back up to arousal before the next orgasm happens. Regardless of the exact rhythm of the orgasms, and whether a person is having two orgasms or ten, the defining feature is continuity. “Multiple orgasms are all about sustaining arousal after the first orgasm’s peak and continuing the pleasure response, instead of shutting it down,” says Howard.

If you think that sounds enjoyable, you’d be correct. But beyond being just plain fun, orgasming multiple times can actually improve your health, says Howard. Each and every orgasm releases feel-good hormones, like dopamine and oxytocin, while simultaneously decreasing levels of cortisol, the stress hormone. Getting multiple doses of those neurochemicals can feel connective, deeply satisfying, and relaxing—even if each climax varies in intensity.

Plus, orgasms release prolactin, a peptide hormone strongly correlated with sleep duration. That post-climax chemical cocktail “really helps you relax and get a much better quality of sleep,” Miriam Sivkin, MD, an ob-gyn in New York, previously told Women’s Health.

But you don’t just benefit from reaching O-town, the journey to get there may also be informative and connective. “[Exploring multiple orgasms] can help people learn their body’s rhythms and increase body awareness,” says Howard. In a partnered setting, the same lessons apply to each other's bodies, which can build more trust and pleasure over time.

In fact, this sexual exploration can even encourage you to redefine pleasure, Howard says. Many people were socialized to treat achieving orgasm like racing toward a finish line. Meaning, you stop as soon as you cross it. But trying to experience multiple climaxes requires you to approach orgasming—and with that, sex more broadly—as something other than an end goal, says Howard. With practice, this can help you and your partner(s) engage with pleasure as something that can unfold slowly and even ebb and flow.

“If you have a body, you’re capable of sexual arousal and can experience multiple orgasms,” says Howard. However, coming again and again usually comes (heh) easier to vulva-owners because they don’t have the same refractory period—essentially a recovery window—after orgasms as most people with penises. The vulvar and vaginal tissues often remain receptive to stimulation after climax, so most women can stay aroused, though sensitivity might spike for a short moment, says Howard. This makes it easier to have another orgasm soon after the first one.

The majority of penis-having people, however, usually need time and distance after an orgasm before pleasure becomes accessible again, she says. Of course, there are no absolutes in sex and pleasure. A penis-owner can train their body to have non-ejaculatory or full-body orgasms—often with the help of breathwork, pelvic floor control, and stimulation—that make multiple orgasms more accessible, says Howard. (If you’ve got questions about the pathway to multiple orgasms for penis-owners, head to this explainer on prostate orgasms, or this one about the male G-spot, as the guide below primarily focuses on making multiple orgasms more accessible for people with vulvas.)

In other words, women’s refractory period is usually marked by a blip of heightened sensitivity, as compared to a full-on stop for many men.

Along your sexual exploration, you may have heard of simultaneous orgasms and blended orgasms. These terms might sound similar, but they describe separate sexperiences.

Multiple Orgasms vs. Simultaneous Orgasms

“Simultaneous orgasms are when two people climax at the same time,” says Howard. With that, two (or more) people are a prerequisite for simultaneous orgasms, whereas multiple orgasms can absolutely occur during a solo session.

Blended orgasms, on the other hand, refer to an orgasm that stems from two or more pleasure centers being stimulated at once—for example, combining clitoral stimulation and vaginal penetration, or clitoral stimulation and anal play.

Sure, both blended and multiple orgasms might be considered amplified versions of the standard O. Still, while blended orgasms are about layering multiple sensations, multiple orgasms involve many separate climaxes, which can all stem from the same type of stimulation repeated.

It all depends on a variety of factors, such as your unique physiology, arousal levels at the time of play, comfort with your own body and your partner, the techniques used, where you are in your menstrual cycle, hydration and stress levels, and more, says Howard. That said, these expert tips can help you set the conditions for multiple orgasms to unfold:

Expectations, as the saying goes, are the thief of joy. And sexually, pressure to perform is the antithesis of arousal. When you focus intensely on achieving orgasm(s), your system shifts into a goal-oriented mode that can make it harder to enjoy the experience—no matter what happens, says Howard.

Research on multiple orgasms is limited, but less than one-fifth of women report having had multiple orgasms in a single session, according to the International Society for Sexual Medicine. So, it might not happen for you, and that’s okay!

Even if you do wind up joining the swath of pleasure-seekers who have had multiple orgasms, there’s no promising it will rock your world. “Each orgasm doesn’t always get more intense; sometimes, the second or third are softer,” says Howard, adding that the point of exploring multiple orgasms isn’t to stack intensity, but to stay tuned into your body.

It is common for people on their multiple orgasm journey to try to get the first orgasm “out of the way,” as if rushing through the first will facilitate the next ones.

Unfortunately, this usually has the opposite effect, says Holly Richmond, PhD, a sex therapist, licensed marriage and family therapist, and author of Reclaiming Pleasure. “Our bodies and nervous systems need to be open and relaxed to achieve multi orgasms.” So, rushing is entirely counterproductive.

Rather than treating orgasm like a yellow light that you need to speed through before it turns red, take your sweet, sweet time building arousal, says Richmond. Let yourself revel in the entire experience, including all the parts and pleasures that preface a (potential) orgasm, she adds.

In practice, this might mean teasing through fabric before stripping naked, making out for eons like teenagers, treating nipple play as the main event, or even just eye-gazing. Whatever it is, when you find something that feels good, stay there—or ask your partner to stay there—just a little longer than you usually would.

Some of the most reliable routes to multiple orgasms are likely hanging out (hopefully, charged!) in your bedside drawer. “Sex toys like vibrators and dildos can help you experience and explore a variety of sensations that the human hand and body just aren’t capable of without them,” says Richmond.

Ultimately, which pleasure products you reach for will depend on your specific preferences. But if you’ve got a clitoral stimulator on hand (e.g., air suction toy, rose toy, classic clit vibe, or rabbit), Richmond suggests starting there.

“The majority of women need intense clitoral stimulation to achieve orgasm,” she says. “In fact, only two percent put something in their vagina for self-pleasure. So it makes sense to offer a little help in this area when pursuing multiple orgasms.”

In general, sex toys feel best when used with lube as this helps the toy slide and glide over your genitals. With rose and other suction toys, a dab of lube can even help the toy recreate the sensation of oral sex. Just be sure to use a water-based lubricant with sex toys made from silicone (which most are), as silicone-based lubricant can degrade the material.

And, even if you usually enjoy high-powered stimulation, start with the lowest setting—or even use it over your underwear—to give your body a chance to build arousal gradually. Remember: It’s a multi-orgasmic marathon, not a sprint.

“If you’re someone who enjoys anal play, adding a dildo, butt plug, or finger can increase overall arousal and make it easier to stay in the orgasmic zone,” says Richmond, making multiple orgasms possible.

While anal stimulation is often talked about as if it is separate from the rest of sexual pleasure, the nerves in and around the butt are interconnected with those throughout the pelvic floor, she says.
